scribed by the Postmaster General. These accounts shall in- 
clude a record of all obligations, investments, expenditures, 
receipts and earnings from any source whatsoever, and shall 
be subject at all reasonable times to inspection and audit by 
the Post Office Department. The Carrier shall furnish to 
the Postmaster General such information regarding the ac- 
counts and operations as he may from time to time require. 


**11. The Carrier shall not directly, or indirectly, promote 
the use of the air mail over the route hereinabove set out, or 
any other air mail route, except in accordance with regula- 
tions prescribed by the Postmaster General. 


‘*12. This certificate is issued upon the express conditions 
that the said Carrier will hold itself subject to all the condi- 
tions imposed by the several Acts of Congress relating to the 
Air Mail Service and to all the provisions of the law relating 
to Post Offices and the Postal Service generally in so far as 
they are applicable to the Air Mail Service; that no Member 
of or Delegate to Congress shall be admitted io any share or 
part of the benefits accruing to it hereunder; and that it has 
not employed a third person to solicit or obtain the same, or 
to cause or procure the same to be obtained upon compensa- 
{fol. 67] tion in any way contingent, in whole or in part, 
upon such procurement; and that it has not paid, or prom- 
ised or agreed to pay, to any third person, in consideration 
of such procurement, or in compensation for service in con- 
nection therewith, any brokerage, commission, or percent- 
age, upon the amount receivable by it thereunder, and that 
it has not, in estimating the price at which it undertakes 
hereunder to carry the mail, included any sum by reason of 
any such brokerage, commission, or percentage, and that all 
money payable to it hereunder is free from obligation to 
any person for services rendered, or supposed to have been 
rendered, in the procurement of this certificate. And it is 
understood that a breach of this condition shall constitute 
adequate cause for the cancellation of this certificate by the 
Postmaster General, and that the United States may retain 
to its own use from any sums due or to become due there- 
under an amount equal to any brokerage, commission, or 
percentage so paid or agreed to be paid.

sons to whom it commits the custody and transportation of

age to the mail through its fault or that of its officers or 
agents, the Postmaster General may make such deductions 
from the pay of the said Carrier as he may deem proper. 


‘*15. The Carrier shall be required to provide necessary 
workroom space in its buildings on the field, or fields, to 
permit proper handling and distribution of mails by Post 
Office Department employees. 


‘*16. This certificate may be cancelled by the Postmaster 
General at any time for willful neglect on the part of the 
holder to carry out any rules, regulations, or orders made 
[fol. 68] for its guidance, notice of such intended cancella- 
tion to be given in writing by the Postmaster General and 
forty-five days allowed the holder in which to show cause 
why the certificate should not be cancelled. 


‘*17. By agreement of the Postmaster General and the 
Carrier, the life of this certificate may be curtailed, said 
certificate may be cancelled, or any other modification may 
be made herein not inconsistent with law. 


“18. The Postmaster General shall have the right at any 
time during the period of service authorized hereby to 
require the Carrier to furnish surety other than that for 
which provision is hereafter made, and in his discretion to 
increase or reduce the amount of the bond that has been fur- 
nished by the Carrier. 


‘19. The delivery to and acceptance and approval by the 
Postmaster General of a bond in the sum of $251,000.00 con- 
ditioned upon the faithful and efficient performance in 
accordance with the terms and conditions hereinbefore set

any and all uses of said lessee, in the handling of its busi-

the sale of gasoline and oils, shall be, considered as a lease 
privilege, and shall be in full force and effect during the 
pendency of this lease and agreement. The lessee agrees 
to have and maintain at said field men and equipment for 
the servicing and repairing of aircraft, but the lessee shall , 
not be required to service or repair aircraft when such F 
servicing or repairing calls for facilities that would not in 
the usual course be maintained on said airport under the 
existent circumstances. The lessor does hereby grant to 
the lessee the exclusive privilege of servicing and/or re- 
pairing aircraft on said airport at reasonable rates.

Liability 


‘“‘The lessee agrees to hold the lessor harmless of and 
from any and all liability arising from injuries to persons 
or damage to property caused by aircraft of the lessee or 
otherwise caused by the lessee when acting in the capacity 
of a user of said airport. 

‘“‘The lessor agrees to hold the lessee harmless of and 
from any and all liability incurred by the lessee by reason 
of the supervision and management of said airport by the 
lessee for and in behalf of the lessor. 

‘‘The lessor agrees to secure agreements from all regular 
users of the airport releasing the lessor and the lessee from 
all liability arising from injuries to the persons or prop- 
erty of said users and indemnifying the lessor and lessee 
against all liability arising from injuries to persons or 
property caused by said users while using said airport. 


Policing of Field 


‘*In the event of the existence of unusual conditions the 
lessor agrees to request necessary policing for said airport 
from County upon request by the lessee. 


Lessor to Prescribe and Enforce Rules and Regulations 


‘‘The lessor agrees to adopt and to enforce rules and 
regulations governing the proper use of said airport and 
[fol. 75] its facilities. The lessor agrees that said rules 
and regulations shall give the right of way to inbound or 
outbound transport and mail planes and that said rules 
and regulations shall not conflict with the rules and regula- 
tions of the Department of Commerce of the United States 
Government.

materials and/or products prior to their acquisition by the 
said lessee.

with the lessee may use said airport under the terms and

{fol.79] ‘‘(a) To establish a public airport on said tract

available for public use as an airport within six months

and improvements as the heads of such departments and

Exuisir C 
‘* Agreement 


‘‘This Agreement, Made in duplicate this first day of 
July, A. D. 1929, by and between the City of Cheyenne, a 
municipal corporation located and existing in the County 
of Laramie and State of Wyoming, hereinafter called the 
‘City,’ party of the first part, and Boeing Air Transport, 
Inec., a private corporation organized and existing under 
and by virtue of the laws of the State of Washington and 
authorized to transact business within the State of Wy- 
oming, hereinafter called ‘Boeing Company,’ party of the 
second part, 

‘*Witnesseth: 

‘‘That whereas, the City is the owner of a municipal 
airplane field located within the corporate limits of Chey- 
enne, which it desires and intends to develop in an effort to 
establish Cheyenne as a center of airplane traffic; and, 

‘‘Whereas, the Boeing Company now operates an air- 
plane line extending from Chicago, in the State of Illinois, 
to San Francisco, in the State of California, passing 
through Cheyenne, which transports passengers, mail and 
express, and hopes to continue such operations; and, 

‘*Whereas, the Boeing Company believes that the provi- 
sions hereof make it advantageous for it to center the op- 
erations of said Chicago-Francisco line at Cheyenne as 
{fol. 81] herein set forth, and the City believes that induc- 
ing the Boeing Company to so center its operations will aid 
in the development of said field as an aviation center, and 
that such development would tend to materially increase the 
prosperity of Cheyenne, 

‘‘Now, Therefore, in consideration of the premises and 
the mutual covenants and agreements herein made and of 
the benefits and advantages which will be obtained by each 
of the parties hereby it is agreed as follows: 


**1, That said municipal airplane field is located in the 
northeast portion of Cheyenne, and that its exact location, 


wel 


———— 
2 YR ARPES SE EEL RL ILO NIRS 


81 


boundaries, etc., are shown by the map ‘Exhibit A’ which 
is hereto attached and hereby made a part hereof. 


‘*2. That said municipal airplane field is at the date 
hereof bounded and surrounded by a line of field lights, 
which are shown on ‘Exhibit A’ and that said field shall 
be immediately enlarged to include all of the area indicated 
on ‘Exhibit A’ as included within the red line, and that the 
words ‘municipal field’ where hereinafter used shall mean 
and denote said enlarged field and all of the portion within 
said red line. That the parties immediately after the en- 
sealing hereof shall proceed to grade and prepare all of 
the new area to be included in said municipal field, and as 
soon as said new area has been properly graded and pre- 
pared shall install a new lighting system marking the 
boundary of said municipal field. The cost of all of the 
work of preparing and grading the new area and the cost 
of the new lighting equipment and the installation thereof 
shall be divided equally between the parties, the total cost 
not to exceed Seven Thousand Dollars ($7,000.00). 


‘*3. The City agrees to use all reasonable efforts to ac- 
quire at its own expense the land included within the area 
which is indicated on ‘Exhibit A’ by the No. 12, and after 
acquiring the same and during the entire period in which 
the Boeing Company shall make any use of said municipal 
field under the provisions hereof that it will keep all of 
said area, or as much thereof as it may acquire, free from

The Witness continuing: 


I have general supervision over the operations of the 
plaintiff from Chicago to San Francisco. I have general 
supervision over the maintenance of the field at Cheyenne, 
as well as all the other fields on our line. 

Plaintiff is engaged in the business of carrying mail, ex- 
press and passengers for hire between Chicago and San 
Francisco and intends to continue in that business. This 
business is carried on by the use of airplanes travelling 
through the air. The fuel used in propelling these air- 
planes is gasoline. We know of no other fuel that could 
be used. A mail compartment is provided in each plane

When we paid the taxes on the gasoline used in interstate 
commerce, we made the payments to avoid the severe pen-

were not made, and we filed a protest with every such pay- 
ment. For example, with the payment made October, 1930, 
we filed a paper in the following words: 


Exuisit D

Alleged Gasoline Tax OF ite $355.60 


Monthly Statement of the Number of Gallons of Gasoline 
Used Exclusively in Interstate Commerce 


By Boeing Air Transport, Inc. (From the Rock Springs 
Air Field, within and outside the State of Wyoming during 
the calendar month ending September 30, 1930, rendered to 
the Treasurer of the State of Wyoming pursuant to the 
provisions of Chapter 73, Session Laws of Wyoming, 1923. 


Shipped to 
Tank consignee and Gallons Amount 
Date car No. Shipped from destination sold of tax 


The Boeing Air Transport, Inc., during the calendar 
month of September, 1930, used exclusively in interstate 
commerce in planes flying from the Rock Springs Air Field 
a total of 8,890 gallons of gasoline on which no gasoline tax 
had previously been paid to the State of Wyoming. A tax 
of four cents a gallon on such gasoline would amount to 
355.60. 

Each and every portion of said gasoline was used solely 
in propelling the airplanes of the Boeing Air Transport, 
Inc., in interstate commerce. A portion of such gasoline 
was used inside the State of Wyoming and a portion outside 
the State of Wyoming.

upon oath that I am Vice-President in charge of operations 
[fol. 92] of the Boeing Air Transport, Inc., a user of gaso- 
line in the State of Wyoming; that the foregoing statement 
is a full, true and correct statement of the number of gal- 
lons of gasoline used in interstate commerce during the 
calendar month ending September 30, 1930, by said Com- 
pany from the Rock Springs Air Field.

The Boeing Air Transport, Inc., is herewith handing to 
you Three Hundred and Fifty-five Dollars and Sixty Cents 
($355.60) claimed by you as taxes on 8,890 gallons of gaso- 
line which was used by said company in flying its interstate 
planes during the month of September, 1930. 

All of the said gasoline was placed by said Company in 
planes engaged in interstate commerce while the planes 
were at the Rock Springs Air Field within the State of 
Wyoming and was used by said planes solely in interstate 
commerce. 

The said Company protests this payment and insists that 
the said taxes are not, nor are any of them due from or 
payable by it, for that all of the said gasoline was used in 
interstate commerce and none of it used for any intrastate 
purpose or otherwise than as above stated. The said Com- 
pany protests that it makes the said payment solely to 
avoid the heavy civil penalties and the heavy criminal 
penalties denounced against it and its officers and servants 
in case of failure to make such payment and only under the 
duress of such heavy liabilities and such penalties. It fur- 
ther gives notice of its intention to bring its action for re- 
covering back each and every amount so paid upon the 
ground that the said tax and every part thereof is an illegal 
exaction from said Company in violation of Article 1, See- 
tion 8 of the Constitution of the United States, being a 
direct burden on interstate commerce, and upon the further

The gasoline used in this interstate commerce was of an 
average value from February 1, 1930, to June 1, 1931, of 
less than 13 cents per gallon. The four cents per gallon 
paid by plaintiff was between 30 and 3314 per cent of the 
value of all the gasoline used. We did not own all of that 
gasoline on April Ist but purchased it during the entire 
period; probably had on hand on April 1st between 5,000 
and 10,000 gallons. We paid the four cents per gallon on 
375,000 gallons of gasoline used in the interstate com- 
merece. We purchased the gasoline. 

The plaintiff pays the administration charges and the 
maintenance charges on the field at Rock Springs. We 
stand al] of the expenses other than any that the City as- 
sumes itself. The City assumes none that I know of. In 
addition to paying the administration and maintenance 
charges at Rock Springs, we pay the City $600.00 a vear 
for the use of the air field and other equipment on the field. 
That is over and above maintaining and administering the 
field and paying all charges. 

The plaintiff does the actual work of administering and 
maintaining the field at Cheyenne. 

In addition to carrying mail and express for hire, plain- 
tiff carries passengers for hire. Plaintiff is a common 
earrier. From February 1, 1930, to June 1, 1931, we have 
earried a total of more than 2,000 passengers through the 
State of Wyoming. During that period there have been 
eighteen passengers between the towns of Rock Springs 
and Cheyenne, some travelling in one direction and some 
in the other. For every passenger within the State of 
Wyoming we have had 131 passengers who passed through 
the State of Wyoming. There is no possibility of appor- 
tioning the gasoline used in these airplanes as between

carrying passengers intrastate and those interstate. The 
additional gasoline required to carry the intrastate pas- i 
sengers is a very small amount, in fact inconsiderable, im- 
possible to determine, 

During the period covered by my testimony, the total 
(fol.95] number of pounds of mail carried into Rock 
Springs from Cheyenne has been 1,173 pounds. That in- 
cludes interstate and intrastate mail. During the same 
period we carried 2,200,000 pounds of mail through the 
State of Wyoming. It was slightly more than that. 

The amount of gasoline required for carrying the addi- 
tional weight of the mail into Rock Springs would be no 
considerable amount. It would be like a man in an auto- 
mobile if he were driving from here to Laramie, if some one 
would hand him a packet of letters and he would stick them 
in his pocket. I do not believe he would burn any move 
vasoline When he got to Laramie on account of the letters 
than if he had not had them. The airplane and the mail and 
the express and the passengers go anyway. 

During the period from February 1, 1930, to June 1, 1931, 
there was no express carried between Cheyenne and Rock 
Springs.

Questions by the Court: 


The gasoline is all stored in one storage tank. When we 
fill a ship engaged in interstate commerce, we have very 
accurate gauges on the pumps, and a very careful record is 
made of the gasoline as drawn out of the tanks. The tax 
is not paid until the gasoline is used. We do not pay on 
what we buy until it is used or disposed of. 


Cross-examination. 


By Mr. Greenwood: 


We procure the gasoline from both inside and outside of 
the State of Wyoming. After we purchase it, part of it is 
brought to Cheyenne and part to Rock Spring. It is stored 
in our tanks at Rock Springs or Cheyenne waiting for use 
at our direction and subject to any use we wish to make of 
it. It is under our control and is property on the field. It 
is not determined at the time it is stored whether it goes 
into interstate commerce or into a ship engaged in that. 
We determine the amount used in interstate and the amount 
applied to other purposes at the time it is used. This 
measurement of the gasoline for the purpose of paying the 
tax is not directed to us by the State. We measure it in 
determining the use it is finally put to, and on that basis 
determine whether we will protest the tax. It is the same 
kind of property, handled in the same way and stored in the 
same place until the final application or use occurs.

Questions by the Court: 


As I understand the State law, the four cent tax goes to 
the town where the gasoline is used. All money collected at 
the Cheyenne airport goes through the State Treasurer’s 
office and then through the City of Cheyenne back to the 
airport. That is my understanding. When we made two 
payments at the same time to the State Treasurer, for ex- 
ample, one of $651.68 and the other of $256.32, one of these 
was for Cheyenne and one for Rock Springs. The reason 
we made the division was because it was two separate ac- 
[fol. 98] counts. We keep a record of the gasoline at Rock 
Springs separately, and Cheyenne the same way, and it is 
a matter of convenience in making our reports to make two 
separate reports. 


Redirect examination: 


The gasoline for which we have accounted as taken at 
Cheyenne is made up of two separate quantities each 
month, one quantity is gasoline put into the tanks in air-

and for washing and for running our engines and experi-

In like manner the gasoline I have testified about as be-

class is gasoline actually used at Rock Springs. None of

[fol. 101] Frank G. Catpweiy, a witness called by the 
plaintiff, testified : 


Since July 1, 1927, I have been and still am general super- 
intendent of the Boeing Air Transport lines. 

The plaintiff's business is to operate airplanes between 
Oakland, California, and Chicago, Illinois, for the purpose 
of transporting mail, passengers and express. The mails 
are carried under the paper Exhibit A over the entire dis- 
tance, operating through Iowa City, Des Moines, Omaha, 
North Platte, Cheyenne, Rock Springs, Salt Lake City, 
Kilko, Nevada, Reno, Nevada, Sacramento, California. 

There is an emergency air field at Laramie. The field at 
Rock Springs is a municipal airport. Our main reason for 
stopping there is for fuel. The business at Rock Springs is 
limited. If it were not for the distance involved, I do not be- 
lieve it would want to step there except for fuel. We stop 
only because of the distanee between Salt Lake and Chey- 
enne, That is an ineidental stop. 


During the period from February, 1930, to June, 1931, we 
carried approximately 1000 pounds of mail for delivery to 
points in Wyoming. That is the whole amount of the mails 
carried for Rock Springs. We also carry express and pas- 
sengers. From February 1, 1930, to June, 1931, there was

The interstate gasoline placed in the tanks at Cheyenne 
was used between Cheyenne and east to Omaha and North 
Platte, and west to Rock Springs and Salt Lake City, and 
that placed in the tanks at Rock Springs was used to Salt 
Lake City and points west, and to Cheyenne and points east. 

The gasoline other than interstate, otherwise called ‘‘lo- 
eal’? was used in trucks, automobiles and washing planes, 
or airplanes, ete. We do a considerable amount of testing. 
An engine is going for that practically all of the time, and, 
[fol. 103] as required by the Department of Commerce, we 
test each airplane where passengers are to be carried after 
each engine installation. That testing is done at the local 
field. 

Kach payment of the four cents tax on interstate gaso- 
line was made under protest. 


(Counsel for defendants agreed that the protest was in 
the form of that introduced in evidence.) 


The tax paid on gasoline used locally was not paid under 
protest. The reason for the payment of the tax of four 
cents per gallon on gasoline used in interstate commerce 
was because of the civil and criminal penalties involved if 
we should refuse to pay. Plaintiff has not had at any time 
at Cheyenne to exceed 7,000 gallons of gasoline in its sup- 
ply tanks. We have paid about $15,000.00 as the four cent 
tax on the gasoline used by the plaintiff in interstate com- 


104 


merce. That would be several hundred per cent of the 
value of the gasoline which we had on hand at any one 
time. It would be between 30 and 3314 per cent of the 
value of all the gasoline that we used during the entire year. 

The plaintiff pays the administration and the mainte- 
nance charges at Rock Springs, and over and above that 
expense pays to Rock Springs $600.00 per year for the 
use of the air field. The maintenance cost for the air field 
at Cheyenne is about $1,000.00 per year. That includes the 
upkeep of the lighting equipment, as well as the upkeep 
of the actual landing field. Upkeep is keeping the field 
true, free from ruts. The chief cause of these ruts is the 
weather, storm and wind. I would estimate that the 
weather or elements cause 75% of the damage that is done 
to the field, and then the landing of various planes causes 
the other 25%. 

The use of the air field at Cheyenne by the plaintiff is 
not exclusive. Others use gasoline there locally. I would 
estimate that there is more gasoline used locally by these 
others than there is by the plaintiff. 

The difference between the value of the right to use this 
gasoline in interstate commerce without paying the tax and 
the value with paying the tax is not less than $50,000.00. 
This $15,000.00 that we have paid on the interstate gaso- 
line includes the time from February 1, 1930, to May 31, 
1931. 


{fol. 104] Cross-examination. 


By Mr. Greenwood: 


No tax has been paid to any other State on the gasoline 
that plaintiff uses in interstate commerce from Cheyenne. 

The gasoline that we sell at Rock Springs is taken from 
our general storage tanks, and that is true also at Chey- 
enne. In speaking of the $50,000.00, the value of our right 
to carry on the interstate commerce without the tax as 
against the right to carry it on with the tax, that figure was 
based on what we have already been required to pay and on 
continuing the payment. Our rates for transporting every 
bit of the commodities, mail, express and passengers, are 
computed upon the basis of yielding a profit, whether we 
paid the tax or not, but the profit will be greater if we do 
not pay the tax.

Questions by the Court: 


We segregate this gasoline for the purpose of making 
the computations as interstate or intrastate through the 
use of a metering device. A record is kept of the gasoline 
used in interstate as well as that used locally or intrastate. 
If we are putting the gasoline into a plane that is about to 
start for Oakland, we keep track of what gasoline we put 
in there. And if it is some plane stationed here and we 
are putting it under test, we keep a record of the actual 
amount of gasoline used on the test. 


Mr. Witu1aM B. Hoare, a witness called on behalf of the 
plaintiff, testified : 


Since July 1, 1927, I have been Superintendent of Repairs 
of the plaintiff. I supervise the repairs of airplanes, lay 
out work for the employees, and sub-supervise the repairs 
of engines, planes and the fields. My part of the work in- 
cludes seeing that the fields are always kept in order and 
well graded and all holes filled up, lighting kept in order, 
and the fences and lights in good condition. 

A man is appointed for three shifts a day to watch it, 
night and day. I am in charge of it. The chief changing 
of the surface of the air field is caused by wind, rain, snow 
and animal life. The landing and taking off of airplanes 
does not affect the surface a great deal except when the 
field is bad or wet. Taking it as a whole, 70 to 75% of the 
[fol. 105] damage to the field is caused by wind and 
weather; perhaps 65 to 70%. Some of the balance by ani- 
mal life. About 20% of the damage is caused by landing 
and taking off of planes. 

Plaintiff’s use of the field is not exclusive. There are 
three other airline operators besides the plaintiff, one local 
concern, and several privately-owned, in addition to what- 
ever transients might go through. I should say plaintiff 
uses three times as much gasoline in intrastate operation 
as all of the other local operators.

Cat Houuiway, a witness on behalf of the defendants, 
testified : 


I am Mayor of Cheyenne, have been in that office for 
eighteen months. Have had some experience in operation 
of airplanes. Am not a licensed pilot. Am a flyer. I am 
connected with an airplane company in this City, the Chey- 
enne Air Service. Its principal place of business is at the 
Cheyenne airport. I have known the conditions at that 
field for about three years and a half. Have seen plain- 
tiff’s airplanes. There are other companies that operate 
there. 

I am familiar with the general use that is made of the 
field by plaintiff’s airships as compared with the others. 
I would say the plaintiff uses the field the most. 

I would say that the testimony of 70 per cent of the deter- 
ioration of the field requiring maintenance is due to the 
elements, rain, snow and wind, is a little bit strong. The 
wear and tear on the airport is caused from moist condi- 
tions and the landing of planes on that soft ground. The 
field is utilized all of the time, in wet weather as well as dry. 

There is a difference in the damage done by a heavy 
plane and a light one. The plaintiff’s airplanes are 40 B’s 
and 95’s and trimotors. The trimotors are single motors. 
The trimotors are the heaviest of the planes that land out 
there. The effect of the landing of one of these planes 
{fol. 108] when the surface is moist is about the same as if 
the wheel of a heavy truck had crossed there. That causes 
damage to the field. The lighter planes do not cause as 
~ much damage as the heavy ones. No Company using the 

_ field uses airplanes as heavy as those just mentioned. 

& If an air field does not have runways enough it must be 
q smooth enough to have an all-runway field in order to 
* become an A1A field. We will have ours smooth enough so 
~ that you can take off in any direction. That is partly the 
‘a purpose of the new acquisition of land for which the bond 
issue has been voted. The rest of the bond issue was for 
the grading and smoothing up of the field in general.

use of gasoline used on this field and in the planes of the

My company pays a tax. And these funds that go to the 
State and then come to the City are included in the airport 
fund. And expenditures made on that field are debited 
and credited to the airport fund. This tax money has come 
into the airport fund of the City and been expended on the 
field and nowhere else. The amount received last vear was 
equal to the amount expended by the City on the field, but 
not this year. In saying that I have in mind the expendi- 
tures of some $7,000.00 in the past for improvements out 
there. IT do not know exactly how much the City has 
received from gasoline tax, 


And on cross-examination the witness testified: 


The gasoline tax received by the City was that part not 
protested by the plaintiff. 

The tax here in controversy, being that on gasoline used 
[fol. 109] in interstate commerce, has not reached the City 
Treasurer, that I know of. 

That $7,000.00 which the City spent in some grading was 
only 50% of the amount that grading cost, and the plain- 
tiff paid the rest, and they both agreed to do just that 
thine as far as I know. 

The time during the course of the year when that field is 
soft from the elements, rain and snow, would probably be 
thirty days ina year. Maybe that is too high, and the rest 
of the time it is not soft at all, and the rest of the time an 
airplane landing there and taking off would make very 
little impression on that surface. T would not say that the 
wind does more damage than the rain to the field. The 


110 


blowing of the wind eddies out holes and makes it rough, 
but not too rough. It makes it rough so that it is not as 
comfortable, and that is one of the things that has to be 
repaired, with loaded ships. For the itinerant flyer it is 
not so bad. The air field is used for the purpose of all 
planes landing and taking off there—Government planes, 
some of them—and some of them are heavier than the plain- 
tiff’s and require the same sort of a smooth air field. 

This maintenance of the field is done in accordance with 
that contract between the City and the plaintiff in carrying 
out that contract, and they have done that. 


O. K. 10-13-31. Richard J. Jackson, Deputy Attorney 
General of Wyoming, one of the attys. for defendant State 
Treasurer. 


O. K. H. B. Henderson, Jr., Atty. for Deft. City of Chey-

ASSIGNMENTS OF Error—Filed Oct. 14, 1931 


Comes now the said plaintiff, Boeing Air Transport, Inc., 
the appellant herein, and says that the decree entered in 
this cause heretofore, to-wit, on the 23rd day of July, A. D.

1931, is erroneous and against the just rights of said plain-

1. The court erred in its finding of fact to the effect that 
the City of Cheyenne has expended substantial sums of 
money in improving its airport landing field and maintain- 
the same, and will be compelled to spend additional sub- 
stantial sums for the same purpose, for that 


(a) The said finding of fact is not sustained by sufficient 
evidence. 


(b) Said finding of fact is so indefinite and uncertain 
that no conclusion of law could be based upon it. 


2. The court erred in its finding of fact ‘‘that said tax of 
four cents per gallon on gasoline imposed upon the plain- 
tiff is likewise imposed upon others similarly situated, with 
the uniform application,’’ for that 


(a) Said finding of fact is upon matter outside the issues 
in said cause and is irrelevant. 


(b) Said finding of fact is not sustained by sufficient 
evidence.

[fol.112] (a) Said finding of fact is outside the issues in 
said cause and is irrelevant. 


(b) The said finding of fact is not sustained by sufficient 
evidence.

JupGE’s MemoranpumM—Filed June 27, 1931 


Kennepy, Judge: 
Decided June 27, 1931. 


[fol. 119] This is a suit in equity in which the plaintiff 
seeks to restrain the enforcement of a state law in regard 
to the collection of a gasoline tax upon the gasoline used 
hy the plaintiff in its operation of an air line in interstate 
commerce in connection with the use of airports at the 
Cities of Cheyenne and Rock Springs in the State of 
Wyoming. The prayer of the bill seeks an interlocutory 
injunction and upon final hearing a permanent injunction 
against the collection of such tax. 

The set-up of the case is one which would seem to bring 
it within the purview of a statutory court of three judges 
(28 U.S. C. A. 380), but as no action was taken by the 
plaintiff to move for a hearing upon an interlocutory 
injunction, the case proceeded to final hearing before a 
single judge without objection on the part of the defend- 
ants and we assume therefore, that the court has juris- 
diction of the proceeding. Seaboard Air Line Railway v. 
Railroad Commission, 213 Fed. 27 (C. C. A. 5). 

The substance of the statutory enactment challenged by 
the plaintiff as being unconstitutional in regard to the 
interstate business of the plaintiff is found in Chapter 14 
of the Session Laws of Wyoming, 1929, Special Session, 
reading as follows: 


‘On and after April 1, 1929, each and every wholesaler 
as defined in this Act, who is now engaged or who may 
hereafter engage in his own name, or in the name of others, 


pM AK Mh tere bages 2 ol 


2 
& 
: 


120 


or in the name of his representatives or agents in this 
State, in the sale or use of gasoline as herein defined shall 
not later than the fifteenth of each month, render to the 
State Treasurer a statement of all gasoline sold or used by 
them in the State of Wyoming during the preceding calen- 
dar month, and pay to the State Treasurer at the same 
time, the license tax of four cents per gallon on all such 
gasoline. Said statement to the State Treasurer shall be 
upon blanks furnished by him, and shall be sworn to by the 
owner or managing agent in the case of an individual, 
firm or association, by the resident general agent or attor- 
ney, in the case of a foreign corporation or by one of the 
principal officers in the case of a domestic corporation. 
Every person, firm or corporation, who shall use any gas- 
oline in this state upon which the said tax has not been 
paid by any wholesaler in this state, shall, on or before 
the 15th day of each calendar month, beginning with the 
[fol. 120] first calendar month after this Act has become 
effective, render a true statement to the State Treasurer, 
duly signed and sworn to, and accompany such statement 
with the payment of a tax of four cents per gallon on all 
gasoline as shown by such statement to have been used by 
him; provided that before making the distribution of funds 
provided for in Section 1 hereof the State Treasurer shall 
pay over all funds received from the gasoline license tax 
on gasoline used at any municipal air field to the city or 
town where such air field is located, to be used for the 
maintenance and improvement of such air field.”’ 


Upon the final hearing testimony was adduced by the 
parties and the following may be considered as a sketchy 
outline of the facts in the ease. 

The plaintiff is a corporation organized and existing 
under the laws of the state of Washington and engaged in 
the operation of an air transport line upon regular 
schedule between Chicago, Illinois, and San Francisco, 
California. In such operation it enjoys a contract with the 
United States government in the transportation of United 
States mails and likewise transports passengers and ex- 
press for hire over its entire line. The substantial portion 
of its business is interstate, in fact the testimony shows 
that its transportation of mails, passengers or express 
within the state of Wyoming is an insignificant percentage 


Ul 
“ 


oF 
S 
Ss 
= 
Re 


a 


of its entire business so that it would be impractical and 
impossible to determine or estimate the relative amount of 
gasoline which would be consumed in carrying on its intra- 
state business. In the operation of its air line it makes 
use of two landing fields owned by the defendant Cities of 
Cheyenne and Rock Springs, respectively, at which points 
its planes are regularly landed on schedule for the dis- 
charge and reception of mail, passengers and express. At 
these points its planes are refueled, examined and condi- 
tioned. Some time after the passage and operation of the 
law which the plaintiff challenges, it began a segregation 
of the gasoline used by it by tolling the amount of such 
gasoline which was used for the operation of its ships, 
traveling in interstate commerce, as distinguished from 
the gasoline which was used in the testing of airships at 
the field, conditioning its motors and sales made to other 
airplane users. As to the first mentioned gasoline, the tax 
of four cents per gallon was paid to the State Treasurer 
[fol.121] under protest in consideration of the high 
penalties fixed for a violation of the statute, but as to the 
latter classification of gasoline the said tax was paid 
thereon to the State Treasurer without protest. During 
the period covering substantially eight months, from the 
15th of March, 1930, to the 15th of November, 1930, the 
plaintiff paid to the State Treasurer in the amount of this 
protested tax, the sum of $5,529.28, representing the tax 
upon gasoline used in interstate commerce at the Cheyenne 
tield and during the same period it paid to the State Treas- 
urer $2,726.32 as representing the tax upon gasoline used 
in interstate commerce at the Rock Springs field. Testi- 
mony was likewise offered as to the continuing payments 
of said protested and unprotested amounts after the filing 
of the bill and substantially up to the final hearing. The 
amount testified to as having been paid of the unprotested 
tax is considerably less than that paid as the protested tax 
at both airports. The City of Cheyenne entered into a 
contract with the plaintiff Company some time in the year 
of 1929 which provided for the use of the municipal air 
field by the plaintiff in the operation of its transcontinental 
air line. The provisions of such contract are numerous 
and not all of them are material to the matter here being 
considered. Among other provisions of the contract it 


aa re Pog OES 


122 


may be said that the plaintiff and the city were to share 
the expense of grading additional land and building a new 
outlining lighting system at a cost of $7,000.00. The city 
agrees to acquire additional land for the airport for which 
it should pay the expense and that it should keep the field 
during the term of the lease (designated as twenty-five 
years), as a well maintained and well regulated airplane 
landing field for the reasonable use of any and all planes 
and airships operated by the paintiff. Im addition, the 
plaintiff agrees to pay the city an annual rental of $1,050.00 
for hangar space and taxi-ways and $3,000.00 for rental of 
several hangars located on the field and owned by the city. 
The city agrees to pay the plaintiff $2,400.00 a vear for the 
general supervision of the field, under the general direction 
of the city. Similar contract relations exist between the 
plaintiff and the city of Rock Springs but of less magni- 
tude owing to the smaller business of the Company at that 
point, but involving a rental there paid by the plaintiff of 
$600.00 per year. At both ports the plaintiff has the privi- 
lege of selling gasoline to the public for airplane purposes. 
As to the city of Cheyenne, the money received from the 
[fol. 122] State Treasurer representing this tax on gaso- 
line used at the airport is placed in an airport fund and 
used exclusively for the purpose of improving and main- 
taining the airfield. During the vear last past not all of 
the money so received and maintained in this separate fund 
had been expended but all of which had been expended was 
expended in connection with the airport. No direct testi- 
mony was offered by the city of Rock Springs as to its 
segregation of this money received from the State Treas- 
urer, except as it may be inferred that the general purpose 
of the law would be carried out by that municipality. In 
Cheyenne, at a recent popular election, a bond issue was 
voted upon and approved by the people authorizing the 
expenditure of $15,000.00 for various purposes in con- 
nection with the improving of its municipal airfield. 
Under these circumstances the plaintiff contends that 
the law requiring the payment of the tax of four cents per 
gallon so segregated and used by it in the operation of its 
air line in interstate commerce is unconstitutional as 
applied to the plaintiff, in that it is in direct contravention 
of the commerce clause of the constitution. Counsel in 


LOO R Sy TTDIIES 


LENE AOSD PTR EINE DLN PIE DAE, 


123 


such contention rely principally upon the case of Helson v. 
Kentucky, 279 U. S. 245, where the state of Kentucky at- 
tempted to tax 75% of the gasoline consumed within the 
limits of Kentucky by a ferry boat operating exclusively 
in interstate commerce. The court through Mr. Justice 
Sutherland at page 248 expresses the gist of the decision 
in the following language: 


‘‘The claim under the commerce clause of the federal 
Constitution was denied on the ground that the tax was 
confined to gasoline used within the limits of the state and 
the commerce clause was not affected. It is with the latter 
question only that we are here concerned. 

Regulation of interstate and foreign commerce is a 
matter committed exclusively to the control of Congress, 
and the rule is settled by innumerable decisions of this 
court, unnecessary to be cited, that a state law which 
directly burdens such commerce by taxation or otherwise, 
constitutes a regulation beyond the power of the state 
under the Constitution.’” * * * ‘The power vested in 
Congress to regulate commerce embraces within its control 
all the instrumentalities by which that commerce may be 
carried on. Gloucester Ferry Co. v. Pennsylvania, supra, 
[fol. 123] p. 204. A state cannot ‘lay a tax on interstate 
commerce in any form, whether by way of duties laid on 
the transportation of the subjects of that commerce, or on 
the receipts derived from that transportation, or on the 
occupation or business of carrying it on.’ Leloup v. Port 
of Mobile, 117 U. 8S. 640, 648; Lyng v. Michigan, 135 U. S. 
161, 166; Ozark Pipe Line v. Monier, 266 U. S. 555, 562. 
While a state has power to tax property having a situs 
within its limits, whether employed in interstate commerce 
or not, it cannot interfere with interstate commerce 
through the imposition of a tax which is, in effect, a tax for 
the privilege of transacting such commerce. Adams Ex- 
press Company v. Ohio, 166 U. S. 185, 218.”” 


Later in the decision at page 252 the following language 
is used: 

‘The statute here assailed clearly comes within the prin- 
ciple of these and numerous other decisions of like charac- 
ter which might be added. The tax is exacted as the price 
of the privilege of using an instrumentality of interstate 


page rosea 


124 


commerce. It reasonably cannot be distinguished from a 
tax for using a locomotive or a car employed in such com- 
merce. A tax laid upon the use of the ferry boat, would 
present an exact parallel. And is not the fuel consumed in 
propelling the boat an instrumentality of commerce no less 
than the boat itself? A tax, which falls directly upon the 
use of one of the means by which commerce is carried on, 
directly burdens that commerce. If a tax cannot be laid 
by a state upon the interstate transportation of the sub- 
jects of commerce, as this court definitely has held, it is 
little more than repetition to say that such a tax cannot be 
laid upon the use of a medium by which such transportation 
is effected. ‘All restraints by exactions in the form of taxes 
upon such transportation, or upon acts necessary to its 
completion, are so many invasions of the exclusive power 
of Congress to regulate that portion of commerce between 
the States.’ Gloucester Ferry Co. v. Pennsylvania, supra, 
p. 214.’’ 


In U. S. Airways v. Shaw, 43 Fed. (2) 148, a statutory 
court in the Tenth Circuit sitting in the Western District 
of Oklahoma decided a case similar in many respects to the 
[fol. 124] case at bar, by declaring the statute invalid. The 
substance of the decision is fairly set forth in paragraphs 
one and two of the syllabus, which read as follows: 


‘State statute levying excise tax on gasoline held in- 
valid as interference with interstate commerce as applied 
to air transportation companies engaged in interstate trans- 
portation business (Const. art 1, * 8, cl. 3; Laws Okl. 1923, 3 
ec. 239, as amended by Laws 1923-24, ec. 101, Laws 19235, e¢. : 
198, and Laws 1929 (Sp. Sess.) ee. 278, 279). 

Complaining parties are engaged in the transportation 
by airplane of passengers, freight, express, and mail from 
points outside to points within the state and vice versa. 
Gasoline used as motor power in airplanes in question is 
purchased largely within state, and tax in question is paid 
through consignees and distributors of gasoline.’’ 

‘*Intrastate operation of air transportation company held 
interdependent with interstate commerce, rendering gaso- 
line used in intrastate business not subject to excise tax 
(Const. art. 1, * 8, el. 3; Laws Okl. 1923, ¢. 239, as amended 
by Laws 1923-24, ec. 101, Laws 1925, ¢. 198, and Laws 1929 
(Sp. Sess.) ec. 278, 279). 


4 


es IIOP nS LLL TINO ME : ed 


Airway companies engaged in interstate transportation 
business carrying passengers, freight, express, and mail 
from points outside to points within state, and vice versa, 
conducted a like intrastate transportation business without 
which interstate transportation business could not be effi- 
ciently and economically conducted.’’ 


: 


Later a statutory court again in the Tenth Circuit sitting 
in the District of New Mexico rendered a similar decision 
in Midcontinent Air Express Corp v. Lujan, 47 Fed. (2) 
266, holding that a tax upon gasoline used by a transport 
corporation in interstate commerce where it was likewise 
engaged in intrastate commerce and incidental to the ‘ 
former, made the entire tax bad. The gist of the decision ; 
is fairly suggested in the second paragraph of the syllabus 
reading as follows: 


‘*Eixcise tax upon all gasoline used by air transport cor- 

poration engaged in interstate commerce and intrastate 
commerce incident») thereto held direct burden on inter- 
state commerce, . therefore invalid (Comp. St. N. M. 
1929, ** 60-101, 60-203). 
[fol.125] Air transport corporation operated from point 
in Texas through state of New Mexico to point in Colorado, 
and engaged in transporting passengers, freight, and ex- 
press for hire. It also carried on intrastate business, but 
such business was merely incidental to interstate business. 
Two classes of business were so interdependent and so com- 
mingled, both with reference to receipts and expenses, that 
amount of gasoline used in each could not be determined 
and apportioned.”’ 


It is not contended that.the entire tax on gasoline used at 
the airports in this ease is invalid, for the reason that it is 
capable of segregation and actually has been segregated as 
to its use in interstate commerce and intrastate commerce 
and only the enforcement of the law which purports to levy 
and collect the tax upon gasoline used in interstate com- 
merce is challenged. 

Were the situation in the case at bar in all respects anal- 
ogous to the cases last cited, it would require no further 
comment from this court as to those decisions ruling this 
case, especially in view of the fact that three of the dis- 
tinguished present Circuit Judges (Cotteral, Phillips and 


aire 


APE PONANL Saal Abc BES AINE P GRRL, NEES 


126 


McDermott) of the Tenth Circuit sat upon the statutory 
courts. But there is another angle of the case which makes 
it less clear to me on account of a peculiar feature of the 
statute in question. The last paragraph of the statute pro- 
vides in substance, that the tax received from gasoline 
used at any municipal airfield be paid by the State Treas- 
urer receiving the same to the city where such airfield is 
located to be used for the maintenance and improvement 
of such field. The query in the mind of the court is as to 
whether or not this does not bring the statute in question 
within the scope of another line of cases presenting a dif- 
ferent feature of the law. The thought can be best ex- 
pressed by a reference to some of these. In Hendrick v. 
Maryland, 235 U. 8. 610, which is a case concerning a grad- 
uated license fee on motor vehicles engaged in interstate 
commerce, Mr. Justice MeReynolds in speaking for the 
court at page 623-624 says: 


‘In view of the many decisions of this court there can 
be no serious doubt that where a State at its own expense 
furnishes special facilities for the use of those engaged 
in commerce, interstate as well as domestic, it may exact 
compensation therefor. The amount of the charges and 
[fol. 126] the method of collection are primarily for de. 
termination by the State itself; and so long as they are 
reasonable and are fixed according to some uniform, fair 
and practical standard they constitute no burden on inter- 
state commerce. Transportation Co. v. Parkersburg, 107 
U. S. 691, 699; Huse v. Glover, 119 U. S. 548, 548, 549; 
Monongahela Navigation Co. v. United States, 148 U.S. 
312, 329, 330; Minnesota Rate Cases, 280 U. S. 352, 405; 
and authorities cited. The action of the State must be 
treated as correct unless the contrary is made to appear. 
In the instant case there is no evidence concerning the 
value of the facilities supplied by the State, the cost of 
maintaining them, or the fairness of the methods adopted 
for collecting the charges imposed; and we cannot say from 
a mere inspection of the statute that its provisions are 
arbitrary or unreasonable.”’ 


Og Ee 


In Postal Telegraph Cable Company v. City of Rich- 
mond, 249 U. S. 252, involving a tax to be paid by a tele- 
graph company engaged in interstate and intrastate busi- 
ness, Mr. Justice Clarke at page 259-260, says: 


ER Ridiakes HRs Seb UR oP F oe : Soars) 4 VRE re RACAA KEN CIE ee, | 


127 


‘‘Even if the net returns from the intrastate business 
should not equal such tax aud it must be paid from inter- 
state earnings, this alone would not be conclusive against 
its validity. If the method of doing interstate business 
necessarily imposes duties and liabilities upon a munici- 
pality, it may not be charged with the cost of these with- 
out just compensation. Even interstate business must 
pay its way,—in this case for its right of way and the ex- 
pense to others incident to the use of it. St. Louis v. West- 
ern Union Telegraph Co., 148 U.S. supra, pp. 98, et seq.; 
Atlantic & Pacific Telegraph Co. v. Philadelphia, 190 U. S. 
160, 163; St. Louis v. Western Union Telegraph Co., 149 
U.S. 465. Such compensation should also inelude the ex- 
pense of inspection of the poles and wires used, and of such 
supervision of the business of the company conducted in 
the streets, as may be reasonably necessary to secure the 
safety of life and property of the inhabitants and of the 
users of the streets; but with the authority in the courts, 
on proper application, to determine whether, under the 
conditions prevailing in a given case, the charge made is 
reasonably proportionate to the service to be rendered and 
{fol. 127] the liabilities involved, or whether it is a dis- 
euised attempt to impose a burden on interstate commerce, 
St. Louis v. Western Union Telegraph Co., 148 U. S. 92; 
St. Louis v. Western Union Telegraph Co., 149 U.S. 465; 
Postal Telegraph-Cable Co. v. Baltimore, 156 U. S. 210; 
Atlantic & Pacific Telegraph Co, v. Philadelphia, 190 U.S. 
160, 163; Western Union Telegraph Co. v. Pennsylvania 
R. R. Co, 195 U.S. 540, 566; Western Union Telegraph 
Co, v. Richmond, 224 U.S. 160, 169.”’ 


In Kane y. State of New Jersey, 242 U. S. 160, involving 
the registration fee of vehicles of non-residents moving 
in interstate commerce, a paragraph of the syllabus reads 
as follows: . 


‘*As applied to vehicles of non-residents moving in inter- 
state commerce as well as to vehicles of residents, the 
amount of the registration fee may properly be based not 
only on the cost of inspection and regulation, but also on 
the cost of maintaining improved roads.’’ 


In Clark v. Poor, 274 U.S. 554, Mr. Justice Brandeis in 
discussing a tax upon motor vehicles operating as common 


128 


carriers exclusively in interstate commerce, at page 556- 
557, says: 


“The plaintiffs claim that, as applied to them, the Act 
violates the commerce clause of the Federal Constitution. 
They insist that, as they are engaged exclusively in inter- 
state commerce, they are not subject to regulation by the 
State; that it is without power to require that before us- 
ing its highways they apply for and obtain a certificate ; 
and that it is also without power to impose, in addition to 
the annual license fee demanded of all persons using auto- 
mobiles on the highways, a tax upon them, under 614-94, 
for the maintenance and repair of the highways and for 
the administration and enforcement of the laws govern- 
ing the use of the same. The contrary is settled. The 
highways are public property. Users of them, although 
engaged exclusively in interstate commerce, are subject 
to regulation by the State to ensure safety and conven- 
ience and the conservation of the highways. Morris v. 
Duby, ante, p. 135; Hess v. Pawloski, ante, p. 352. Users 
of them, although engaged exclusively in interstate com- 
merece, may be required to contribute to their cost & up- 
[fol. 128] keep. Common carriers for hire, who make the 
highways their place of business, may properly be charged 
an extra tax for such use, Hendrick v. Maryland, 235 U.S. 
610; Kane v. New Jersey, 242 U. 8S. 160. Compare Pack- 
ard v. Banton, 264 U, S. 140, 144.°’ 


Later the same Justice in Sprout v. City of South Bend, 
277 U. S. 168, in discussing a license fee imposed upon 
those engaged in interstate commerce at page 169 says: 


‘Tt is true that, in the absence of federal legislation 
covering the subject, the State may impose, even upon 
vehicles using the highways exclusively in interstate com- 
merece, non-diseriminatory regulations for the purpose of 
insuring the public safety and convenience; that licensing 
or registration of busses in a measure appropriate to that 
end; and that a license fee no larger in amount than is 
reasonably required to defray the expense of administer- 
ing the regulations may be demanded. Hendrick v. Mary- 
land, 235 U. S. 610, 622; Kane v. New Jersey, 242 U.S. 
160; Morris v. Duby, 274 U. S. 185; Clark v. Poor, 274 U. 
S. 554. Compare Hess v. Pawloski, 274 U. S. 352. These 


ASIEN Mar eh TNL Mh. Rig Li aoe tl 


129 


powers may also be exercised by a city if authorized to 
do so by appropriate legislation. Compare Erb vy. Morasch, 
177 U.S. 584, 585; Mackay Telegraph Co. v. Little Rock, 
250 U. S. 94, 99. Such regulations rest for their validity 
upon the same basis as do state inspection laws, Patapsco 
Guano Co. vy. Board of Agriculture, 171 U. S. 345; Red 
“C?? Oil Co. v. Board of Agriculture, 222 U. S. 380, and 
municipal ordinances imposing on telegraph companies, 
though engaged in interstate commerce, a tax to def "ay 
the expense incident to the inspection of poles and wires, 
Western Union Telegraph Co, v. New Hope, 187 U. 8, 419: 
Postal Telegraph-Cable Co. v. Richmond, 249 U. S. 252, 
208; Mackay Telegraph Co. v. Little Rock, 250 U.S. 94, 99.°° 


The most recent pronouncement of the high court called 
to my attention, is that found in Interstate Transit Co., 
Ine. v. Dick Lindsey, decided April 13, 1931. In that case a 
tax imposed by the State of Tennessee on busses engaged 
in interstate commerce was held unconstitutional. The 
basis of the decision, however, is the fact that the Tennes- 
see statute was upon its face not enacted for any purpose 
[fol. 129] looking to the construction and maintenance of 
the state highways, but was merely a privilege tax for the 
carrying on of interstate business. The pertinent portion 
of the opinion setting forth legal principles which may be 
said to apply to the ease at bar is as follows: 


“While a State may not lay a tax on the privilege of en- 
gaging in interstate commerce, Sprout v. South Bend, 277 
U.S. 168, it may impose even upon motor vehicles engaged 
exclusively in interstate eammerce a charge, as compensa- 
tion for the use of the publie highways, which is a fair con- 
tribution to the cost of constructing and maintaining them 
and of regulating the traffic thereon. Kane v. New Jersey, 
242 U.S. 160, 168-169; Clark v. Poor, 274 U. S. 554: Sprout 
v. South Bend, supra, pp. 169-179. As such a charge is a 
direct burden on interstate commerce, the tax cannot be 
sustained unless it appears affirmatively, in some way, that 
it is levied only as compensation for use of the highways or 
to defray the expense of regulating motor traffic. This may 
he indicated by the nature of the imposition, such as a mile- 


9—971 


RIE GER MD PITRE RIN TERE eT 


130 


age tax directly proportioned to the use, Interstate Busses 
Corp. v. Blodgett, 276 U. S. 245, or by the express allocation 
of the proceeds of the tax to highway purposes, as in Clark 
v. Poor, supra, or otherwise. Where it is shown that the 
tax is so imposed, it will be sustained unless the taxpayer 
shows that it bears no reasonable relation to the privilege 
of using the highways or is discriminatory. Hendrick v. 
Maryland, 235 U. 8, 610, 612; Interstate Busses Corp. v. 
Blodgett, 276 U. S. 245, 250-252. Compare Interstate 
Busses Corp. v. Holyoke Street Ry., 273 U.S. 45,51. But 
the mere fact that the tax falls upon one who uses the high- 
way is not enough to give it presumptive validity.”’ 


Applying the principle of these decisions to the facts and 
the statute in the case at bar, it seems to me, it falls within 
such principle and must be ruled accordingly. Certainly 
there can not be said to be substantial distinction because 
in the ease at bar the tax is not assessed by the municipal- 
itv but is assessed under a state law and later turned over 
to the municipality for the particular use designated. The 
result to the plaintiff is, of course, the same. Tere, the 
é tax on the gasoline used by the plaintiff in interstate com- 
merce is returned directly to the municipality for the main-

[fol. 180] tenance and improvements of the air fields which 

the plaintiff is using and in the end results in a benefit to 
# the plaintiff. Under the logic of the decisions above cited, 
¢ even interstate commerce must assist in paying its own 
3 way. The evidence in the case shows beyond peradven- 
g ture, that the much greater portion of the airplane traffic 
i at these municipal ports is that of plaintiff in the operation 
a of its interstate planes. Presumably, therefore, it would 
® secure the greater benefit in the matter of enlargement, im- 
d provement and maintenance of the fields at high standard. 
ia Neither can we see any way to make a substantial dis- 
= tinction for the purposes of the question here, between the 
ae maintenance of city streets or public highways as discussed 
Fs in the Supreme Court cases and an airplane landing field 
a open to all planes for general use. The facility of good

larger in amount than is reasonably necessary to carry on 
the legitimate purpose for which it is intended. Sprout v. 
South Bend, supra. As to the amount of such tax as com- 
pared with its use to the purposes intended, it depends 
largely upon the judgment of the taxing authority, so long 
as the rule of reason is in control. Hendricks v. State of 
Maryland, supra. Neither is reasonableness of the tax to 
he measured merely by the value of the land oceupied, or 
the profits of the business, or by the amount expended in 
any particular year. Western Union Telegraph v. New 
Ilope, 187 U.S. 419. 

Considering the entire evidence in this case, taking into 
account all the expenses that the municipalities have here- 
tofore had, are having and contemplate having in the future 
in the matter of improving their municipal airports, I am 
not prepared to say that the tax so challenged is not fairly 
commensurate with the general purposes for which it is 
intended. We all know that municipalities throughout the 
land are continually expending from time to time many 
thousands of dollars in acquiring, perfecting and improv- 
ing their airports in the matter of keeping pace with the 
great forward strides of air transportation. It seems but 
fair that all this should be taken into consideration in de- 
termining the issues in this state. The airports of the 
future must be made great terminal facilities to acecommo- 
[fols. 131 & 132] date this ever increasing traffic and any 
tax received and devoted exclusively to this purpose will un- 
doubtedly in the stretch of time be inadequate to meet the 
reasonable demands of extension and improvement. 

For the reasons stated, findings, conclusions and a decree 
may be submitted, dismissing the bill at plaintiff's cost and 
reserving to if proper exceptions.

Opinion —Oct. 4, 1932 


Lewis, Circuit Judge, delivered the opinion of the court: 


Appellant, a corporation under the laws of the State of 
Washington, brought this suit seeking an injunction 
against the State Treasurer of Wyoming restraining him 
from collecting from appellant a license tax imposed by 
the statutes of Wyoming on gasoline to be used by appel- 
lant in its airplanes in the transportation of passengers, 
express and United States mail in interstate commerce, 
and to recover the tax theretofore paid. In appellant’s 
business its planes, carrying both intrastate and interstate 
commerce, ply between Chicago, Illinois and Oakland, Cali- 
fornia, and by written agreements with Cheyenne, Wyom- 
ing, and Rock Springs, Wyoming, the planes land on mn- 
nicipal fields, at each of those places and take on gasoline, 
that being the fuel used to generate the needed power in 
flights. 

The bill charges that the Wyoming statute is an-inter- 
ference with and a burden on that commerce in violation 
of Clause 3, Section 8, Article I of the Constitution of the 
United States, and is therefore illegal and void, ‘‘in that 
the said statutes purport to impose and levy, and do im- 
pose and levy a tax on all gasoline purchased or consumed 
as fuel in Wyoming to propel airplanes flying between the 
said State and neighboring States in interstate commeree, 
thus exacting the said tax as the price of the privilege of 
using an instrumentality of interstate commerce and thus 
evading the exclusive power of Congress to regulate com- 
merece between the several states.’’ It is further charged: 


‘that under the provisions of said statutes the wholesale 
distributors or consignees of gasoline are made agents of 
the State of Wyoming for the purpose of collecting said 
vasoline tax, said agents acting under the direction of the 
defendant, State Treasurer, and while the said distributors 
and or consignees of gasoline actually collect, return and 
remit said tax to the State of Wyoming and to the de- 
fendant, the State Treasurer, said gasoline tax is in law 
and in practice levied and imposed against the consumer of 


134 


said gasoline within the State of Wyoming and not against 
the wholesale distributor or consignee, and such tax is paid 
[fol. 136] by all such consumers, ordinarily direct to the 
defendant, the Treasurer of the State of Wyoming, which 
said tax this plaintiff has been forced to pay in order to 
purchase or consume any gasoline in the State of Wyo- 
ming, and will hereafter be forced to pay until defendant, 
his agents, servants, employees, workmen and other per- 
sons acting by and through him are enjoined and re- 
strained from collecting the same by order of this court. 
. * * 

‘*Plaintiff further avers that * * * the plaintiff in 
order to continue to operate its said airplanes in the con- 
duct of its said business is compelled to purchase from time 
to time, and from day to day, large quantities of gasoline 
in the State of Wyoming, and that no such distributor or 
any other dealer in said State will sell such gasoline to the 
plaintiff unless the plaintiff so purchasing the same shall 
make payment of the said tax levied and assessed thereon 
at the time of the purchase thereof and that hence the 
plaintiff is unable to purchase any gasoline in the State of 
Wyoming for the operation or propulsion of its said air- 
planes without at the time paying the said tax thereon, 
and plaintiff alleges that by the express provisions of said 
Acts and or by the practical operation thereof by the de- 
fendant and the legal effect thereof the said tax is ab- 
sorbed in the price paid for all gasoline purchased in 
Wyoming for use or consumption in said State by the pur- 
chaser, including this plaintiff, and that under the provi- 
sions of said Acts the said tax, although required to be 
collected and returned to the State by the distributor of 
or dealer therein as the agent of the State, is both in prae- 
tice and in legal effect a tax on the consumer of such gaso- 
line re ee 


It is further alleged in the bill: 


‘That a part of such gasoline used by the plaintiff is 
purchased by and delivered to the plaintiff in the State of 
Wyoming; that a part thereof is purchased outside of said 
State; and that such gasoline purchased and delivered to 
the plaintiff either in or outside of said State of Wyoming 
is used and consumed by it as fuel for the propulsion of its

yas 


ro"

oh | 


said airplaines in its interstate commerce and business as 
aforesaid, and is an instrumentality of interstate com- 
merce.”’ 


[fol. 137] That there has been exacted of it, and it has paid 
said tax amounting to several thousand dollars on gasoline 
so used by appellant in interstate commerce, whether pur- 
chased or delivered to it in the State of Wyoming or else- 
where, under protest, because of threatened criminal and 
civil penalties authorized by the statute for non-payment. 

The statute was first passed in 1923. It levied a tax 
of one cent on each gallon of gasoline sold or used within 
the state. It has been amended at every subsequent session. 
We think it plain that the original act and as amended 
imposes the tax only against wholesalers as that term is 
defined in the original act. Section 1 of the original act 
(Session Laws, Wyoming, 1923, Chapter 73) defined whole- 
salers thus: 


‘¢*Wholesaler’? is hereby defined as any person, firm, or 
corporation who imports or causes to be imported gasoline, 
as herein defined, for sale in the State of Wyoming to the 
jobber or consumer, or to the persons, firms, corporations, 
or associations of persons, who, in turn, sell to the jobber 
or consumer. The term ‘wholesaler’ is further defined to 
mean any person, firm, or corporation who produces, re- 
fines, manufactures, blends, or compounds gasoline in the 
State of Wyoming for use, sale or distribution in this 
State.”’ 


Section 3 required that every wholesaler, as defined in 
the act, who was then engaged or should engage in the sale 
or use of gasoline, should not later than the fifteenth of 
each month render a statement to the State Treasurer show- 
ing the amount of gasoline sold or used in the state during 
the preceding calendar month, and to pay to the State 
Treasurer at the same time the license tax of one cent 
per gallon on all such gasoline. That act also required 
that every wholesaler should keep a record of all purchases, 
receipts, sales, and distribution of gasoline, and that the 
funds received from the license tax by the State Treasurer 
should be used for the maintenance of highways. Section 6 
of the original act made it unlawful for any wholesaler to

sell or offer gasoline for sale while delinquent in the pay-

civil and criminal, for violations of that provision. The 
amount of the tax was thereafter raised at subsequent ses- 
mings until it was four cents per gallon prior to and at the 
tim this suit was brought. 

‘fol. 188] Section 3 of the original act was amended at 
the session of 1927. It did not change the requirement of 
the original act that the wholesaler should pay the tax, 
but that amendment further provided: 


‘*Every person who shall use any gasoline in this state 
upon which the said tax has not been paid by any whole- 
saler in this state, shall, on or before the twenty-fifth day 
of each calendar month * * * render a true statement 
to the State Treasurer duly signed and sworn to, and ac- 
company such statement with the payment of the tax of 
three cents per gallon on all gasoline, as shown by such 
statement to have been used by him.”’
