{fol. 139] vidual, firm or association, by the resident gen- 
eral agent or attorney, in the case of a foreign corporation 
or by one of the principal officers in the case of a domestic 
corporation, Every person, firm or corporation, who shall 
use any gasoline in this State, upon which the said tax 
has not been paid by any wholesaler in this State, shall, 
on or before the 15th day of each calendar month, be- 
vinning with the first calendar month after this Act has 
become effective, render a true statement to the State 
Treasurer, duly signed and sworn to, and accompany such 
statement with the payment of a tax of four cents per 
vallon on all gasoline as shown by such statement to have 
been used by him; provided that before making the dis- 
tribution of funds provided for in Section 1 hereof the 
State Treasurer shall pay over all funds received from 
the gasoline license tax on gasoline used at any municipal 
air field to the city or town where such air field is located, 
to be used for the maintenance and improvement of such 
air field.’? 

The latter half of said section 3, supra, as amended at 
the special session beginning with the words ‘‘ Every per- 
son, firm or corporation, who shall use any gasoline in this 
State, upon which the said tax has not been paid by any 
wholesaler in this State,’’ makes it plain, we think, that 
the tax is primarily imposed against a wholesaler. 

In substance and effect the facts here are not different 
from those in the case of Eastern Air Transport, Ine. v. 
South Carolina Tax Commission, 285 U. S. 147. In that 
case the Transport Company complained that a license tax 
of six cents a gallon, imposed on those who sold gasoline, 
was required to be paid by it for gasoline used in its planes 
in interstate commerce when it purchased the gasoline in 
South Carolina, and that the six cents was a direct burden 
upon interstate commerce. The court said: 


‘eo 
S 

a 
& 
é 


os os 
re 


satel Titi 


ese 


138 


‘‘So far as the present question is concerned, the dis- 
tinction (between an excise tax and a property tax) is not 
important. If such a license tax for the privilege of mak- 
ing sales within the State were regarded as in effect a tax 
upon the goods sold, its validity could not be questioned 
in the circumstances here disclosed, as in that aspect the 
tax would be upon a part of the general mass of property 
within the State and hence subject to the State’s authority 
[fol. 140] to tax, although the property might actually be 
used in interstate commerce. * * * There is no sub- 
stantial distinction between the sale of gasoline that is used 
in an airplane in interstate commerce and the sale of coal 
for the locomotives of an interstate carrier, or of the loco- 
motives and cars themselves bought as equipment for inter- 
state transportation. A non-discriminatory tax upon local 
sales in such cases has never been regarded as imposing 
a direct burden upon interstate commerce and has no 
greater or different effect upon that commerce than a gen- 
eral property tax to which all those enjoying the protection 
of the State may be subjected.’’ 


Appellant in argument and brief puts emphasis on the 
word ‘‘use’’ found in the latter part of section 3, supra, 
and says it is a use tax, and the use was in interstate 
commerce, relying on Helson v. Kentucky, 279 U. S. 245. 
It may be conceded that the forepart of this section im- 
poses a use tax against the wholesaler on that part of 
the gasoline that it consumes; but that is not the tax in- 
volved here. The tax here under consideration is the 
license tax against the wholesaler exacted of him for the 
privilege of carrying on the business of selling gasoline, 
and when appellant made its purchases it had an option 
under the statute of then paying the tax added to the pur- 
chase price, or of assuming its payment and accounting to 
the State Treasurer therefor after it had used the gasoline. 
It chose to do the latter and thus discharged a primary 
obligation of the wholesaler. This case is wholly unlike 
Helson’s case. There the gasoline was purchased in Illinois 
for a ferry boat engaged in interstate commerce, and the 
tax involved was held to be a use tax imposed by a Ken- 
tucky statute on that part of the gas consumed by the ferry 
boat while within Kentucky. The court said, pages 249, 
252: 


eee SET OTR ENS EE 


BS APR 7 


139 


‘‘While a State has power to tax property having a situs 
within its limits, whether employed in interstate commerce 
or not, it cannot interfere with interstate commerce through 
the imposition of tax which is, in effect, a tax for the privi- 
lege of transacting such commerce. * * * The tax is 
exacted as the price of the privilege of using an instru- 
mentality of interstate commerce. It reasonably cannot 
be distinguished from a tax for using a locomotive or a 
‘ar employed in such commerce. A tax laid upon the use 
of the ferry boat, would present an exact parallel.’ 


[fol. 141] So we conclude that appellant’s attack upon the 
Wyoming statute cannot be sustained. 

So far we have had in mind only gasoline purchased in 
Wyoming. The bill alleges that a part of the gasoline 
purchased and used by appellant in its planes in interstate 
commerce was purchased outside of Wyoming. The testi- 
mony on that subject is this: Appellant procures gasoline 
from both inside and outside the State of Wyoming; after 
it is purchased part of it is brought to Cheyenne and part 
to Rock Springs and stored in tanks for use at appellant’s 
direction and subject to any use it wishes to make of it; 
there is one tank at Rock Springs and two at Cheyenne; 
the capacity of one of the tanks at Cheyenne is five thou- 
sand gallons and the other two thousand gallons; appellant 
sells soine gasoline at Cheyenne and at Rock Springs; it 
displays a price sign at each place, stating the price of 
gasoline including the tax; other planes use both of these 
fields, and appellant uses gasoline locally at each field in 
trucks , automobiles, for testing purposes, and washing 
planes; there is no separate receptacle at either field in 
which appellant stores the gasoline which it sells to other 
airships, or buys outside Wyoming; it is all put into the 
storage tanks; there are gauges on the pumps, and a record 
Was kept each time that gasoline was drawn from the tanks; 
the amount used locally by appellant, or sold, is in this 
way kept separately from the amount used by appellant 
in its interstate flights; it likewise measures the gasoline 
put into its planes for interstate commerce use. The record 
does not disclose how much gasoline during the times in 
question was purchased outside the state. On the facts 
stated it must have been mingled in the tanks with gasoline 


nal 


ds PARE RAE TNT RIERA 


2 TET OES 


140 


purchased within the state. Appellant paid the tax on the 
gasoline used by it locally and on that sold to others with- 
out protested payments on all put in its tanks and used 
in interstate flights. Obviously it is impossible to ascertain 
how much gasoline that came from outside of Wyoming 
was thus used in its planes. Moreover, it does not appear 
whether gasoline purchased outside of Wyoming was de- 
livered to appellant in Wyoming or outside the state. 
Judges McDermott and Pollock are agreed however that 
the pleadings and evidence are sufficient to require an 
injunction restraining collection of the tax on gasoline 
obtained by appellant on purchases made outside Wyoming 
and then brought into the state and used in its planes in 
interstate flights. 

[fol. 142] The Wyoming Legislature at its general session 
in 1929 passed another act (Chapter 89) relating to the 
sale and delivery of gasoline. Section 1 of that act requires 
that every person, firm, corporation, association, co-part- 
nership, or common carrier transporting, conveying or 
bringing gasoline into the state of Wyoming for retail or 
wholesale consumption or distribution within the state shall 
furnish to the State Treasurer not later than the 10th of 
each calendar month a verified statement showing the num- 
ber of gallons of gasoline delivered within the State of 
Wyoming during the preceding month, giving the name of 
the person, firm, corporation, association, or co-partnership, 
to whom the same was delivered and the place of delivery. 
It is further provided that: 


‘*Every wholesaler in gasoline and every person, firm, 
corporation, association, co-partnership, or common carrier 
transporting, conveying, or bringing gasoline into the State 
of Wyoming for retail or wholesale consumption or dis- 
tribution within the State of Wyoming, as in this act de- 
fined, shall keep a record of all purchases, receipts, sales, 
and distribution of gasoline.’’ 


It further provided that such record shall at all times 
be subject to inspection by the State Treasurer. A penalty 
is provided for failure to make such report. No data rela- 
tive to the amount of gasoline procured by appellant from 
outside the state required by this act is found in the record. 


141 


It is therefore impossible to even approximate the amount 
of gasoline coming from the outside during the time in 
question or the conditions of purchase and delivery. Con- 
ceding that appellant might make purchase of gasoline out- 
side the state under given circumstances, bring that gasoline 
to Cheyenne and Rock Springs and use it in interstate 
flights and not be subject to account to the State Treasurer 
for the statutory tax on gasoline so used, it is the writer’s 
opinion that the pleadings and facts in this case are too 
meager and indefinite to require and sustain a ruling and 
decree thereon. 

The proviso to said section 3 of this act was put into 
that section at the special session of 1929 after the City 
of Cheyenne had entered into a written contract with ap- 
pellant by which the latter obtained the right for a con- 
sideration to use the city’s airplane field for twenty-five 
years commencing July 1, 1929, in landing, taking off, and 
there putting their planes in condition. The right was not 
fol. 145] exclusive. Other planes could be given and were 
given similar rights. The city had made valuable improve- 
ments, as has appellant, at their respective costs. Appel- 
lant agreed to pay a specified amount in annual rentals, 
and additional costs of named future improvements and 
maintenance and management of the field were to be appor- 
tioned between them in a way stated in the contract. The 
city agreed that appellant should be entitled to the full use 
of the field during the lease on terms as favorable as might 
he granted by the city to other users, and that no charge 
should be exacted from appellant for any use which it might 
make additional to that specified in the contract, and that 
the city would impose no charge of any kind or character 
for materials or other products purchased or otherwise ac- 
quired by appellant for use on the premises leased. Later 
the city authorized a bond issue of $15,000.00 for enlarge- 
ment of the field and for improvements such as grading, 
leveling, fencing, ete. Appellant contends that the proviso 
fo section 3 impairs the obligations of the contract between 
the city and appellant in that it is in violation of Section 
10, Article I of the United States Constitution. The proviso 
reads thus: 


‘‘provided that before making the distribution of funds 
provided for in Section 1 hereof the State Treasurer shall

Foe EE he Se SEHD LENE OBESE BIS GIB ERED RIS ARI DAT A a I

pay over all funds received from the gasoline license tax 
on gasoline used at any municipal air field to the city or 
town where such air field is located, to be used for the 
maintenance and improvement of such air field.’’ 


We are not impressed with this contention. It should be 
said it is made in response to statements of the trial judge 
that the tax on gasoline used at the field and turned over to 
the city pursuant to the proviso might be regarded as com- 
pensation to the city for the facilities it furnished and its 
outlay in maintenance and upkeep of the field. The trial 
judge relied on Sprout v. City of South Bend, 277 U. S. 163, 
and others like it. But it will be seen from what has been 
said we do not rest our conclusion on that ground. The 
obligations of the contract were in no way affected by the 
proviso. It provided a fund with which the city’s obliga- 
tions might be discharged, in part at least, but without it all 
of the tax would have been applied on the State’s highways. 
Appellant was not injured by the change and cannot com- 
plain. Hooker vy. Burr, 194 U.S. 417. 

The District Court denied any relief by injunction and 
[fols. 144-146] dismissed the bill. In so far as the court 
denied the writ prayed for the case will be remanded with 
directions to issue the writ enjoining collection of the tax 
on all gasoline procured by appellant on purchases com- 
pleted outside the State of Wyoming and then brought into 
that state and used in its planes in interstate commerce. 
In all other respects the order of dismissal of the District 
Court is affirmed. The costs on appeal will be divided, each 
party will be adjudged to pay the costs that it or they in- 
curred. 

It is so ordered.

Petition ror Cuarirication or Decree—Filed Nov. 14, 1932 


Counsel for Boeing Air Transport, Inec., appellant in the 
above entitled cause, respectfully make the following repre- 
sentations as grounds for the clarification of the decree 
indicated by the opinion filed in said cause on October 4, 
1932.

144 


The decision of this court reverses the decree entered by 
the court below, in part, and directs the issuance of a writ 
of injunction against the exaction of the Wyoming gasoline 
tax with respect to such gasoline as appellant purchases 
outside of the State and brings into the State for use in 
fueling its airplanes in interstate commerce. This part of 
the decision reads as follows: 


‘*The District Court denied any relief by injunction and 
dismissed the bill. Insofar as the court denied the writ 
prayed for the case will be remanded with directions to 
issue the writ enjoining collection of the tax on all gaso- 
line procured by appellant on purchases completed outside 
the State of Wyoming and then brought into that state and 
used in its planes in interstate commerce. In all other 
respects the order of dismissal of the District Court is 
affirmed.”’ 


Earlier in the opinion, it is said: 


orp 


he record does not disclose how much gasoline during 
the times in question was purchased outside the state. On 
the facts stated it must have been mingled in the tanks with 
gasoline purchased within the state. Appellant paid the 
[fol. 148] tax on the gasoline used by it locally and on that 
sold to others without protested payments on all put in its 
tanks and used in interstate flights. Obviously it is impos- 
sible to ascertain how much gasoline that came from out- 
side of Wyoming was thus used in its planes. Moreover, it 
does not appear whether gasoline purchased outside of 
Wyoming was delivered to appellant in Wyoming or out- 
side the state. Judges McDermott and Pollock are agreed, 
however, that the pleadings and evidence are sufficient to 
require an injunction restraining collection of the tax on 
gasoline obtained by appellant on purchases made outside 
Wyoming and then brought into the state and used in its 
planes in interstate flights.’’ 


We are in doubt as to whether or not the qualified rever- 
sal of the decree forecloses appellant’s right to make proof 
and recover in the court below such taxes as were paid 
under protest upon gasoline purchased outside of the State 
of Wyoming and used in fueling its airplanes in interstate 
commerce, 


LP Lea 


145 


It is true the record before the court does not show any 
segregation of the taxes paid under protest with respect to 
those paid on gasoline used in interstate commerce but pur- 
chased outside the State of Wyoming and those paid on 
gasoline within the State of Wyoming. It does not follow 
that such segregation cannot be made. 

The court has assumed that during the entire period dur- 
ing which appellant made tax payments under protest, 
which tax payments appellant seeks to recover in this 
action, gasoline purchased within the State of Wyoming 
was mingled with gasoline purchased without the State of 
Wyoming. Although it does not appear in the record, in 
view of this assumption of the court, appellant feels that it 
is permissible to say that during the entire year 1930 all of 
the gasoline used or sold by appellant in Wyoming was 
purchased by the appellant outside of the State ef Wyo- 
ming and thereafter imported by appellant into that state. 
Consequently, appellant is in a position to make definite 
proof that taxes in excess of $10,000 were paid by appellant 
on gasoline purchased by appellant outside of Wyoming 
and thereafter used by it in propelling its planes in inter- 
stale commerce. 

The bill of complaint contains a prayer for other and 
[fol. 149] further relief. Under the principle announced 
in The Salton Sea Cases, 172 Fed. 792, certiorari denied 
215 U.S. 604, this prayer is sufficient to support a decree 
awarding recovery of such taxes as were paid under protest 
with respect to gasoline purchased outside of the State of 
Wyoming and used by appellant in said state solely in fuel- 
ing its airplanes in interstate commerce, which taxes this 
court now holds have been unlawfully exacted. 

This court has granted to the appellant some relief— 
namely, an injunction against the collection of the tax on 
gasoline purchased outside of the State of Wyoming and 
used in said state in fueling appellant’s airplanes in inter- 
state commerce. Where injunctive relief is given by the 
appellate court, but the record before that court is not suffi- 
cient to determine the extent of the monetary relief which 
follow the injunctive relief, the proper practice is for the 
appellate court to remand the case to the trial court with 
instructions to take testimony for the purpose of determin- 


10—9571 


see Pr ERRERCeN in | 


Seach a Palit 


Mt A eT 


146 


ing the monetary award to which the successful party is 
entitled. This is the practice followed by the Supreme 
Court and we submit should be followed by this court in 
this case. 


Dowagiae Mfg. Co. vs. Minnesota Moline Plow Co., 
235 U.S. 641. 
Hassal vs. Wilcox, 130 U.S. 493. 


We accordingly ask that the decision of the court be clari- 
fied by specifically recognizing appellant’s right to apply 
to the lower court for the recovery of such taxes as have 
been paid by it under protest with respect to gasoline pur- 
chased outside the State of Wyoming and used in fueling 
its airplanes in interstate commerce. 


Respectfully submitted, John W. Lacey, Lacey & 
Loomis, Cheyenne, Wyoming. William M. Allen, 
Eugene C. Luccock, Todd, Holman & Sprague, 
Seattle, Washington, Attorneys for Appellant.

WILLIAM H. EDELMAN, State Treasurer ;

Petition for Writ of Certiorari 
To the Honorable The Supreme Court of the United States: 


The petition of William H. Edelman, State Treasurer: 
Harry R. Weston, State Treasurer: The City of Cheyenne, a 
municipal corporation; and The City of Rock Springs, a mu- 
nicipal corporation, respectfully shows to this Honorable 
Court: 


A 
Issues in the Case 


1. On the 25th day of November, 1930, the respondent, 
Boeing Air Transport, Inc., a corporation of the State of 
Washington, filed in the United States District Court for the 
District of Wyoming, its Bill of Complaint against your peti- 
tioners, residents of the State of Wyoming, in which said Bill 
is sought relief permanently enjoining and restraining peti- 
tioners from collecting a license, or excise, tax of four cents 
per gallon on all gasoline held and owned by it in the State of 
Wyoming and used as fuel in propelling its airplanes in the 
transportation of passengers, express, and United States mail 
in interstate commerce. 


OLE MEET AERP SM 


2 


2. The grounds for the relief sought are that the statutes 
of the State of Wyoming by virtue of which the tax had been 
collected from respondent are unconstitutional and in viola- 
tion of Clause 3 of Section 8 of Article I of the Constitution 
of the United States, the commerce clause of the Federal Con- 
stitution, and that the tax is on the use of gasoline in inter- 
state commerce, and thus an illegal interference with com- 
merce among the states. 

3. It is alleged that, by reason of the severe provisions and 
- penalties of the statutes, irreparable loss and damages would 
E result to respondent unless it should be granted equitable re- 
; 


lief. 

4. The alleged illegal tax had been paid by respondent, un- 
der protest, and the amount thereof is alleged to be in excess 
of $10,000.00, in the original and supplemental bills. 

5. It is alleged that the gasoline of respondent on which 
the tax had been paid had been purchased by respondent part- 
ly within and partly outside the State of Wyoming. 

6. The prayer of the Bill is in the usual terms for a perma- 
nent injunction, and for, to quote therefrom, ‘‘such other and 
further relief as the equity and justice of the case may require 
and which the Court may deem meet and just, whether spe- 
cifically prayed for herein or not’? (R. 1 and 44). 

7. Petitioners answered, relying upon the legality of the 
statutes under which the tax had been collected, and asserting 
that the tax was not an illegal interference with, or regulation 
of, commerce among the states (R. 13, 17 and 30). 


SI MOOPRS RST I 


B 
Opinion of the Court, and Final Decree 


The matter of the issuance of an interlocutory injunction 
was not pressed, no such injunction was issued, and the action 
was tried by a single District Judge. 

On trial on the merits, the findings of fact (R. 47), conelu- 
sions of law (R. 59), and decree (R. 60) of the District Court 
were in favor of petitioners, the injunction was denied, and 
the case was ordered dismissed at costs of respondent. 

In due time the respondent appealed the case to the United 
States Circuit Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit, which 
~ Court, in its opinion, ordered the case remanded with direc- 
— tions to issue the writ enjoining collection of the tax on all 


C= BUST Le EE ER PET LIS 


gasoline procured by respondent on purchases completed out- 
side the State of Wyoming and then brought into the State 
and used in its planes in interstate commerce, but in all other 
respects affirmed the order of dismissal of the District Court. 
However, the Senior Circuit Judge was of the opinion that 
the pleadings and facts in the case were too meager and in- — 
definite to require and sustain a ruling and decree thereon. 
The opinion of the Cireuit Court of Appeals is reported 
in 61 Fed. (2d) 319, Advance Sheets No. 5, November 
98, 1932 (R. /ZH ). Decree was entered on October 20, 1932, 
remanding the cause to the Trial Court with directions to issue 
the writ of injunction enjoining petitioners from collecting the 
tax of four cents per gallon on all gasoline procured by re- 
spondent on purchases of gasoline completed outside the State 
of Wyoming and then brought into said State and used in pro- 
pelling its airplanes in interstate commerce (R. /4YA/_ ). 

In due time petitioners filed in the Circuit Court of Appeals 
their petition, and supporting brief, for a rehearing, which was 
denied by the Court on November 25, 1932 (R./47_ ). 

In due time respondent filed in the Circuit Court of Appeals 
its petition for clarification of decree, which was denied by the 
Court on November 25, 1982 (R./37 ).

Statement of Facts 


And your petitioners respectfully show that the facts of the 
matter involved and on which the United States Cireuit Court 
of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit rendered its decree directing 
the issuance of the writ of injunction against the collection of 
such tax by petitioners are, in the language of that Court, Page 
322, the following: 

‘‘Appellant procures gasoline from both inside and 
outside the State of Wyoming; after it is purchased part 
of it is brought to Cheyenne and part to Rock Springs and 
stored in tanks for use at appellant’s direction and sub- 
ject to any use it wishes to make of it; there is one tank 
at Rock Springs and two at Cheyenne; the capacity of one 
of the tanks at Cheyenne is five thousand gallons and the 
other two thousand gallons; appellant sells some gasoline 
at Cheyenne and at Rock Springs; it displays a price sign 
at each place, stating the price of gasoline including the

tax; other planes use both of these fields, and appellant 
uses gasoline locally at each field in trucks, automobiles, 
for testing purposes, and washing planes; there is no sep- 
arate receptacle at either field in which appellant stores 
the gasoline which it sells to other airships, or buys out- 
side Wyoming; it is all put into the storage tanks; there 
are gauges on the pumps, and a record was kept each time 
that gasoline was drawn from the tanks; the amount used 
locally by appellant, or sold, is in this way kept separately 
from the amount used by appellant in its interstate flights ;

been mingled in the tanks with gasoline purchased within 
the state. Appellant paid the tax on the gasoline used by

purchased outside of Wyoming was delivered to appellant 
in Wyoming or outside the State.”’

Brief in Support of Petition for Writ of Certiorari 


L. 
The Opinions of the Courts Below 


The opinion of the District Court is reported in 51 Fed. (2d) 
130, Boeing Air Transport, Ine. vs. Edelman, State Treas- 
urer, et al., and is at page 118 of the Record. 

The opinion of the United States Cireuit Court of Appeals 
for the Tenth Circuit is reported in 61 Fed. (2d) 319, Boeing 
Air Transport, Ine. vs. Edelman, State Treasurer, et al., and 
is at page /3H of the Record. 


Il. 
Jurisdiction 


1. Paragraph (a) of Section 240, Act of February 15, 1925, 
Chapter 229, 43 Statutes at Large 936 (U.S. Code, Title 28, 
Section 347 (b)) is as follows: 

‘*Tn any ease, civil or criminal, in a cireuit court of ap- 
peals, or in the Court of Appeals of the District of Colum- 
bia, it shall be competent for the Supreme Court of the 
United States, upon the petition of any party thereto, 
whether government or other litigant, to require by 
certiorari, either before or after a Judgment or decree by 


such lower court, that the cause be certified to the Su- 
preme Court for determination by it with the same power 
and authority, and with like effect, as if the cause had been 
brought there by unrestricted writ of error or appeal.’’ 


2. The date of the decree to be reviewed is October 20, 1932 
(R. /44 ). The petition for rehearing was denied Novem- 
ber 25, 1932 (R. /Y ). 

3. The parties ar€é citizens of different states and the mat- 
ter in controversy exceeds in value the jurisdictional amount. 

The action is in equity in which respondent, Boeing Air 
Transport, Inc., a corporation, seeks to permanently enjoin the 
State Treasurer of the State of Wyoming from collecting an 
excise tax of four cents per gallon on all gasoline held and 
owned by it in the State of Wyoming and used as fuel in pro- 
pelling its airplanes in the business of interstate ecommerce, 
no tax having been previously paid thereon, a part of such 
gasoline having been purchased within the State of Wyoming 
and a part thereof having been purchased outside the State, 
but all thereof stored before its use in respondent’s storage 
tanks at the cities of Cheyenne and Rock Springs in said 
State, and thereafter withdrawn from such tanks and con- 
sumed as fuel in propelling respondent’s airplanes in the busi- 
ness of interstate commerce. 

The tax is collected on or before the 15th of the months fol- 
lowing its use. 

The ground on which respondent relies for injunctive relief 
against the collection of the tax is that the statutes of the 
State of Wyoming, providing for the payment of such tax, are 
in violation of Clause 3 of Section 8 of Article I of the Con- 
stitution of the United States, and is an illegal interference 
with commerce among the states. 

The opinion of the United States Cireuit Court of Appeals 
for the Tenth Cireuit sustains the tax as legal on all such 
gasoline procured by respondent on purehases within the 
~ State of W voming and holds the tax to be illegal on all sueh 
gasoline procured by respondent on purchases outside the 
State of Wyoming and then brought into said state and used 
in propelling its airplanes in interstate commerce. 

The decree of the United States Cireuit Court of Appeals 
for the Tenth Cireuit remands the cause to the Lower Court, 
with directions to issue the writ of injunction prayed for, en- 
» joining collection of the tax of four cents per gallon on all gas-

‘ Argument 

é THE STATUTES OF WYOMING LEVY A LICENSE 


TAX OF FOUR CENTS PER GALLON ON ALL GASO- 
LINE USED OR SOLD FOR DOMESTIC CONSUMPTION, 
AFTER ITS STORAGE IN THE STATE, AND DO NOT 
TAX THE USE OF GASOLINE AS A MEANS BY WHICH 
INTERSTATE COMMERCE IS EFFECTED. 


The statutes of the State of Wyoming under which the pe- 
titioner, State Treasurer, is collecting and enforcing the tax 
of four cents per gallon on the gasoline of respondent, are 
found in Article 11 of Chapter 115, Wyoming Revised Statutes, 
1931, page 1862, and are set forth in full as an appendix to this 
brief. 

We direct particular attention to the following provisions 
thereof: 

**715-1102. Amount of tax. A license tax of four cente 
per gallon is hereby levied on all gasoline used or sold 
in this state for domestic consumption to be collected as 
hereinafter set forth.”’

**115-1105. Payment of tax. * * * Every person, firm or 
corporation, who shall use any gasoline in this state upon 
which the said tax has not been paid by any wholesaler in 
this state, shall, on or before the 15th day of each calendar 
month, render a true statement to the state treasurer, duly 
signed and sworn to, and accompany such statement with 
the payment of a tax of four cents per gallon on all gaso- 
line as shown by such statement to have been used by 
him: * & #99 


The statute of 1923 taxed gasoline sold for domestic con- 
sumption. The word ‘‘used’’ was added in 1925 and the two 
words, ‘‘used’’ and ‘‘sold’’, have uniformly appeared there- 
after. 

As there has been no attempt to construe the statute as per- 
mitting the taxing of gasoline placed in appellant’s planes in 
an adjoining state and used or consumed while passing 
through Wyoming, but solely as permitting the taxation of 
gasoline stored by appellant in its tanks and thereafter placed 
in its planes within the state, under well established prin- 
ciples of construction followed by this Court, this construction 
will he adopted and followed. 

As no attempt is being made by the taxing authorities to 
tax gasoline placed in planes or vehicles of transportation 
without the state, it may be assumed that the word ‘‘used’’ 
was added to the statute for the purpose of making it uni- 
form and to prevent its evasion rather than for the purpose of 
adding new sources of revenue. No doubt, the intention of 
the legislators of 1923 was to levy a tax on all gasoline which 
had attained a fixed situs within the state for the purpose of 
domestic consumption, by levying the same on all gasoline 
sold therein, but knowing the possibility of its evasion as well 
as desiring to place all domestic consumers on an absolutely 
equal basis, the word ‘‘used’’ was added by the succeeding 
legislature, and is to be construed as synonomous with sale. 

We thus are dealing with a statute, as interpreted and eon- 
strued, which levies a tax of four cents per gallon on all gaso- 
line, which has aequired a fixed situs within the State of Wyo- 
ming and is thereafter and within the State sold to a domestic 
cousumer or placed by a domestic consumer in his machine 
for use therein. A single tax on all gasoline for domestie con- 
sumption, whether sold or used, not a tax on the sale and 
another on the use. 


a 
4 


B 

INSTRUMENTALITIES USED ININTERSTATE 

COMMERCE, UNDER THE DECISIONS OF THIS 

COURT, ARE SUBJECT TO EITHER A PROPERTY, OR 

LICENSE TAX BY A STATE, THE SAME AS OTHER 
PROPERTY, ALL HAVING A SITUS THERKIN. 


‘*It has been again and again said by this court that 
while no state could impose any tax or burden upon the 
privilege of doing the business of interstate commerce, 
yet it had the unquestioned right to place a property tax 
on the instrumentalities engaged in such commerce.”’ 
Cleveland, C. C. & St. L. R. Co. v. Backus, 154 U. S. 439, 
445. 

‘*While a state has power to tax property having a situs 
within its limits, whether employed in interstate com- 
merce or not, it cannot interfere with interstate commerce 
through the imposition of a tax which is, in effect, a tax 
for the privilege of transacting such commerce.’’ Helson ; 
v. Commonwealth of Kentucky, 279 U. S. 245, 249. ; 

‘‘The appellant emphasizes the fact that the tax has 
been construed by the Supreme Court of the State to be 
an excise tax and not a property tax. Gregg Dyeing Co. 
v. Query, Supreme Court of South Carolina, decided April 
13, 1931, S.C. , 164 S. KE. 588. So far as the pres- ; 
ent question is concerned, the distinction is not important. 
If such a license tax for the privilege of making sales with- 
in the State were regarded as in effect a tax upon the 
goods sold, its validity could not be questioned in the cir- 
cumstances here disclosed, as in that aspect the tax would 
be upon a part of the general mass of property within the 
State and hence subject to the State’s authority to tax, 
although the property might actually be used in interstate 
commerce. ‘It is elementary,’ said the Court in New Jer- 
sey Bell Teleph. Co. v. State Bd. of Taxes & Assessments, 
280 U.S. 338, 346, 74 L. Ed. 468, 467, 50S. Ct. 111, ‘that 
a State may tax property used to carry on interstate com- 
merce.’ Treating the tax as an excise tax upon the sales 
does not change the result in the instant case, as the sales 
are still purely intrastate transactions. Superior Oil Co. 
v. Mississippi, 280 U. S. 390, 395, 74 L. Ed. 504, 507, 50 
S. Ct. 169. Undoubtedly, purchases of goods within a 
State may form part of transactions in interstate com- 


Ao IO LAF AE Oe 


GEA IYE LAM ARS 


EE PRR ION 


Pe RS 


RTS SRA EAA EEL ATER Pe ARIE 


13 


merce and hence be entitled to enjoy a corresponding im- 
munity. But the mere purchase of supplies or equipment 
for use in conducting a business which constitutes inter- 
state commerce is not so identified with that commerce as 
to make the sale immune from a non-discriminatory tax 
imposed by the State upon intrastate dealers. There is no 


substantial distinction between the sale of gasoline that 3 
is used in an airplane in interstate transportation and the | 


sale of coal for the locomotives of an interstate carrier, 
or of the locomotives and cars themselves bought as equip- 
ment for interstate transportation. A non-discriminatory 
tax upon local sales in such cases has never been regarded 
as imposing a direct burden upon interstate commerce and 
has no greater or different effect upon that ecommerce 
than a general property tax to which all those enjoying 
the protection of the State may be subjected.’’ Eastern 
Air Transport, Inc. v. South Carolina Tax Commission, 
et al., 285 U.S. 147, 152, 153. 


Cc 

PROPERTY ACQUIRED BY PURCHASE IN AN- 
OTHER STATE THAN THAT IN WHICH IT IS USED, 
WHENEVER IT BECOMES A PART OF THE GENERAL 
MASS OF THE PROPERTY OF THE STATE OF ITS 
USK, BY DELIVERY AND STORAGE THEREIN, BE- 
COMES SUBJECT TO THE TAXATION LAWS OF THE 
STATE OF ITS USE AND SITUS, REGARDLESS OF 
THE CLASS OF THE TAX OR THE USE THEREAFTER 
OF THE PROPERTY. 


The tax was sustained by the Cireuit Court of Appeals on 
all gasoline of respondent procured by it on purchases within 
the State. 

Gasoline of respondent procured on purchases outside the 
State, having thereafter been stored by it in its storage tanks 
within the State, was thereafter withdrawn, as and when 
needed, placed in its airplanes, and used and consumed in pro- 
pelling its airplanes in interstate commerce. We think a li- 
cense tax on all such gasoline is equally not open to attack as 
an interference with commerce among the states. 

We can conceive of no difference between the two, so far as 
respondent’s objection thereto is concerned. Neither is a tax

on the use of gasoline in interstate commerce, nor a tax ‘‘ex- 
acted as the price of the privilege of using an instrumentality 
of interstate commerce.’’ 

Whenever the subjects of interstate commerce cease longer 
to be in transportation between the states, that is, as repeated- 
ly said by this Court, ‘‘come to rest’’ within a state, the sub- 
jects of such commerce then become a part of the mass of the 
property of that state, subject to its laws, and attain a situs, 
subject to taxation within that state. 

‘“That with respect to such substances when they have 
passed out of interstate commerce and have come to rest 
within the state and become a part of the general mass 
of property or have become the subject of domestic com- 
merce, the state may impose inspection fees substantially 
in excess of the cost of inspection, and thus make them 
a source of general revenue, is also free from doubt, other 
than any which may arise from its own constitution.’’ 
Texas Co. v. Brown, 258 U. S. 466, 475, 66 L. Ed. 721. 

**Qur conclusion must depend on the answer to the ques- 
tion: Is this a regulation of, or a burden upon, interstate 
commerce? We think it is neither. The oil had come to 
a state of rest in the warehouse of the appellants, and had 
become a part of their stock, with which they proposed to 
do business as wholesale dealers in the state. The inter- 
state transportation was at an end, and whether in the 
original package or not, a state tax upon the oil as prop- 
erty, or upon its sale in the state, if the state law levied 
the same tax on all oil or all sales of it, without regard to 
origin, would be neither a regulation nor a burden of 
the interstate commerce of which this oil had been the 
subject.’’ Sonneborn Brothers v. Keeling, et al., 262 U.S. 
506, 508, 509, 67 L. Ed. 1095. 

‘Interstate transportation having ended, the taxing 
power of the state in respect of the commodity which was 
the subject of such transportation, may, so far as the 
commerce clause of the federal Constitution is concerned, 
be exerted in any way which the state’s constitution and 
laws permit, provided, of course, it does not discriminate 
against the commodity because of its origin in another 
state.’’ Hart Refineries v. Harmon, 278 U.S. 499, 501, 502, 
73 L. Ed. 475. 


es ESTELLE AIL NE ELE 
15 


As controlling and upholding the legality of the tax, we 
cite Gregg Dyeing Co. v. Query, et al., 286 U. 8. 472 (Prelim- 
inary Print No. 3), 76 L. Ed. 852, opinion by Mr. Chief Justice 
Hughes, and affirming the opinion of the Supreme Court of 
S. C. as reported in 164 8. KE. 588. At pages 478 and 479 it is 
said: 

‘*We may lay aside, as not here involved, any question 
relating to importations from foreign countries. As to 
interstate commerce, the questions are (1) whether the 
Act as applied by the state court imposes a direet burden 
upon that commerce, and (2) whether, although the sub- 
ject of the tax would otherwise be within the power of 
the State, the tax is invalid beeause it creates an uncon- 
stitutional discrimination against transactions in. inter- 
state commerce. 

‘‘As to the first question, we are not concerned with 
what the tax is called but with what the statute does. It 
imposes an exaction with respect to gasoline purchased 
in other States and brought into South Carolina and there 
placed by appellants in storage for future use within the 
State. By the terms of the Act, as construed by the state 
court and applied to these appellants, interstate ecommerce 
in relation to the subject of the tax has ended. The gaso- 
line has come to rest within the State, having been placed 
in appellants’ storage tanks and added to appellants’ 
property kept for local purposes. In such cireumstances 
the State has the authority ‘to tax the produets or their 
storage or sale.” Texas Company v. Brown, 258 U. S. 
466, 478; Sonneborn Bros. v. Cureton, 262 U.S. 506, 519, 
920; Hart Refineries v. Harmon, 278 U. 8S. 499, 501, 502. 
Not only may loeal sales of gasoline thus brought into 
the State be taxed, but its use as well. This was speeifical- 
ly determined in Bowman vy. Continental Oil Co., 256 
U.S. 642, 648, 649. See Hart Refineri cay v. Harmon, supra; 
Breece Lumber Co, v. Asplund, 283 U. S. 788. There is an 
exception in the case of a tax inal on use in interstate 
commerce, as on use in interstate transportation. Helson 
v. Kentucky, 279 U. S. 245, 252: Eastern Air Transport 

*. South Carolina Tax Comm., 285 U. 8. 147. In view of 
bia well-established principles, we find no ground for 
concluding that the State could not impose the tax with 
respect to the gasoline of appellants which was kept with- 


ait cas | 


Se Stehi SRE: 


16 


in the State for use in their local enterprises. As the 
Court said, in Hart Refineries v. Harmon, supra, inter- 
state transportation having ended, the taxing power of 
the state in respect to the commodity may, so far as the 
commerce clause of the Federal Constitution is concerned, 
‘be exerted in any way which the State’s constitution and 
laws permit’. This, of course, is on the assumption that 
the tax does not discriminate against the commodity be- 
cause of its origin in another State.”’ 


Bearing on the legality of the tax, the only difference be- 
tween the two statutes, as construed, is that the South Caro- 
lina statute ‘‘does not propose to tax any gasoline until 
twenty-four hours after it has lost its interstate character.”’ 
That the length of time the property has been stored, or has 
been ‘‘at rest’’, within the state is not the controlling feature, 
3 we quote from an importation case, as in point on the ques- 
— tion of the length of time the property must be ‘‘at rest’’ be- 
— fore it is subject to the taxing power of the state. 
: “‘Without further reference to authorities we state our 
conclusion to be that within the decision in Brown vy. 
: Maryland the boxes, cases or bales in which plaintiffs’ 
B goods were shipped were the original packages, and the 
goods imported by them lost their distinctive character 
as imports and became a part of the general mass of the 
property of Louisiana, and subject to local taxation as 


& other property in that state, the moment the boxes, 
z cases or bales in which they were shipped reached their 
3 destination for use or trade and were opened and the 


separate packages therein exposed or offered for sale; 

consequently, the assessment in question was not in viola- 

3 tion of the Constitution of the United States.’’ May v. 
New Orleans, 178 U.S. 496, 509, 510, 44 L. Ed. 1165. 


— In Foster & Creighton Co, v. Graham, et al., 154 Tenn. 412, 
3 285 S. W. 570, 47 A. L. R. 971, a tax on gasoline received in 
; interstate commerce and stored and used was sustained, al- 
~ though the statute contains no provision fixing any definite 
~ length of time for loss of its interstate character. 

In Burke v. Bass, et al., 242 N. W. 606, decided by the Su- 
_preme Court of Nebraska May 20, 1932, a tax on gasoline was 
sustained, under the same conditions as in the Tennessee case.

In Breece Lumber Co., et at. v. Mirabal, 34 N. M. 643, 287 
Pac. 699, a tax on gasoline was sustained, under the same eon- 
ditions as in the Tennessee and Nebraska cases. After the 
substitution of Asplund as defendant, as reported in 283 U. S. 
788, the decree of the state court was affirmed without opinion. 

‘Tt is not the intent with which the gasoline is to be 
used that determines the liability of the taxes, but it is 
the status of the gasoline at the time.’’ American Air- 
ways, Ine. v. Wallace, et al., 57 Fed. (2d) 877, 880. 

See, also, Brown v. Houston, 114 U. S. 622, 632-634; 

American Steel & Wire Co. v. Speed, 192 U. S. 500, 520; 

Bowman v. Continental Oil Co., 256 U. S. 642, 648-649; 

Jerome H. Sheip Co. v. Amos, 130 So. 699 (Fla.). 


While in the Helson case, supra, the tax, levied by the State 
of Kentucky on gasoline purchased in Tlinois, was deelared 
illegal, the gasoline never at any time came to rest in Ken- 
tucky and never became part of the mass of the property of 
that state. Obviously that case is distinguishable from the in- 
stant case, both, on principle, and, as clearly shown by the 
Gregg Dyeing Co. case, supra, and other cases cited herein. 


TRA OLS — | 


18 


Conclusion 


It is, therefore, respectfully submitted that this case is one 
calling for the exercise by this Court of its supervisory pow- 
ers, in order that the State of Wyoming may proceed with the 
enforcement of its statutes and the collection of the license tax 
on gasoline, as provided therein, and that to such an end a writ 
of certiorari should be granted and this Court should review 
the decision of the United States Cireuit Court of Appeals for 
the Tenth Circuit. 

Respectfully submitted,

Chapter 115, Article 11, Wyoming Revised Statutes, 1931,

GASOLINE TAX 


115-1101. Definitions. The words and phrases used in this 
article shall be construed for the purpose thereof as follows: 

(1) ‘*Wholesaler’’ is hereby defined as any person, firm or 
corporation who imports or causes to be imported gasoline, as 
herein defined, for sale in the state of Wyoming to the jobber 
or consumer, or to the persons, firms, corporations or associa- 
tions of persons, who, in turn, sell to the jobber or consumer ; 
the term ‘‘wholesaler’’ is further defined to mean any person, 
firm or corporation who produces, refines, manufactures, 
blends or compounds gasoline in the state of Wyoming for 
use, sale or distribution in this state; 

(2) ‘‘*Gasoline’’ is hereby defined as the volatile substance 
produced from petroleum, natural gas, oil shales or coal, here- 
tofore sold under the name of gasoline, and such other volatile 
and inflammable liquids produced or compounded for the pur- 
pose of operating or propelling motor vehicles, except the 
product commonly known as kerosene. [L. ’23, ¢. 73, § 1. 


REE IES VEES 


115-1102. Amount of tax. A license tax of four cents per 
gallon is hereby levied on all gasoline used or sold in this state 
for domestic consumption to be colleeted as hereinafter set 
forth. [L. 29, ¢. 139, §1, amending L. ’27, c. 70, §1; L. ’25, e. 
89, §1; L. 723, e. 73, §2. 


115-1103. Payment of tax. Each and every wholesaler as 
defined in this article, who is now or who may hereafter en- 
gage in his own name, or in the name of others, or in the name 
of his representatives or agents in this state in the sale or use 
of gasoline as herein defined shall not later than the fifteenth 
of each month, render to the state treasurer a statement of 
all gasoline sold or used by them in the state of Wyoming dur- 
ing the preceding calendar month, and pay to the state treas- 
urer at the same time, the license tax of four cents per gallon 
on all such gasoline. Said statement to the state treasurer 
shall be upon blanks furnished by him, and shall be sworn 
to by the owner, or managing agent, in the case of an individ-

paid, and in such suit the court may, upon application made by 
the state of Wyoming, appoint a receiver of the property and 
business of the defendant, for the purpose of impounding the 
same as security for the payment of said delinquent tax and 
any judgment recovered in said suit. The state treasurer, or 
his assistant or deputies, shall have the right, during business 
hours to inspect the books and records of any party subject to 
said tax, for the purpose of determining the amount of tax due 
and payable and whether the same has been paid. Any party

SUPREME COURT OF THE UNITED STATES 
OCTOBER TERM, 1932 


No. 571 


WILLIAM H. EDELMAN, State Treasurer; 
HARRY R. WESTON, State Treasurer; 
THE CITY OF CHEYENNE, ET AL., 
Petitioners 
vs. 


BOEING AIR TRANSPORT, INC. 
Supplement to Brief in Support of Petition 


Correction 


There is an error in the printed decision of the Circuit 
Court of Appeals, quoted in the petition for writ of certiorari. 
In line 17, page 4, following ‘‘without’’, there should be in- 
serted, ‘‘protest, but’’. The sentence should read, ‘‘ Appellant 
paid the tax on the gasoline used by it locally and on that sold 
to others without protest, but protested payments on all put 
in its tanks and used in interstate flights’’. (R. 140) 


Argument 


Opinion of the District Court is at page 119 of the Record. 

Opinion of the Circuit Court of Appeals is at page 132 of 
the Record. 

Decree of the Circuit Court of Appeals is at page 142 of the 
Record. 

We think that the decision of this Court announced Feb- 
ruary 6, 1933, in No. 176, The Nashville, Chattanooga & St. 
Louis Railway v. Roy C. Wallace, Comptroller, et al., estab- 
lishes the correctness of propositions B and © of the Argu- 
ment of petitioners’ brief in support of the petition, and the 
legality of an excise tax on gasoline consumed in interstate 
commerce, under the facts disclosed by the Record in the case 
at bar. Indeed, although not controlling, there is one stronger

~ fact in the instant case. Respondent sold a portion of the 
' gasoline stored in its tanks and from which it withdrew the 


gasoline on which it is seeking to enjoin the collection of the 
tax and contracted with the City of Rock Springs for the right 
of such sale. 

Proposition ‘‘A’’ of petitioners’ brief is as follows: 


“THE STATUTES OF WYOMING LEVY A LICENSE 
TAX OF FOUR CENTS PER GALLON ON ALL GASO- 
LINE USED OR SOLD FOR DOMESTIC CONSUMPTION, 
AFTER ITS STORAGE IN THE STATE, AND DO NOT 
TAX THE USE OF GASOLINE AS A MEANS BY WHICH 
INTERSTATE COMMERCE IS EFFECTED.”’ 


The Wyoming statutes are given in full as an appendix to 
brief of petitioners in support of their petition, beginning at 
page 19. 

The Cireuit Court of Appeals said, ‘‘ We think it plain that 
the original act and as amended imposes the tax only against 
wholesalers as that term is defined in the original act’’. (R. 
135) 

That the Wyoming gasoline tax primarily is intended to be 
collected from and paid by the wholesaler, as defined in the 
law, we think is true. 

Chapter 14 of the Special Session of 1929 amended Section 
2 of Chapter 139, Session Laws of 1929 (Section 115-1103, 
W. R.S., 1931), by adding at the end thereof the following: 


‘Every person, firm or corporation, who shall use any 
gasoline in this state upon which the said tax has not been 
paid by any wholesaler in this state, shal’, on or before 
the 15th day of each calendar month, render a true state- 
ment to the state treasurer, duly signed and sworn to, 
and accompany such statement with the payment of a tax 
of four cents per gallon on all gasoline as shown by such 
statement to have been used by him; provided, that be- 
fore making the distribution of funds provided for in 
Section 115-1108, the state treasurer shall pay over all 
funds received from the gasoline license tax on gasoline 
used at any municipal er county air field to the city, town 
or county where such air field is located, to be used for 
the maintenance and improvement of such air field.”’ 


prea eo Ate, 
ogee 


ae 


Thus this amendment provides for three distinct things: 

(1) Levies the tax on ‘‘Every person, firm or corpora- 
tion’’, coming under its provisions, and not defined as a 
‘‘wholesaler’’ ; 

(2) Provides for the payment of the tax; and, 

(3) Provides that the tax on ‘‘all gasoline used at any 
municipal * * * air field’’ shall be paid over to the city 
where located ‘‘for the maintenance and improvement of such 
air field’’. 

The only other difference between the Wallace case and the 
ease at bar is as to the wording of the state statutes. The 
Tennessee statute imposes the tax on ‘‘storing any of the 
products mentioned in this act, and distributing the same, or 
allowing the same to be withdrawn from storage, whether 
such withdrawal be for sale or other use’’. By the amend- 
ment the Wyoming law levies the tax ‘‘on gasoline used at 
any municipal or county air field’’, and by the preceding 
Section ‘‘on al] gasoline used or sold in this state for do- 
mestic consumption’’. 

In the Wallace case it was decided that a tax on gasoline 
purchased outside the state of Tennessee and stored within 
the state, withdrawn and used in generating motive power in 
interstate commerce, was a valid tax, under the statute of 
that state. 

Therefore the only question in the case at bar is, Is the tax 
valid under a statute levying the same ‘‘on all gasoline used 
* * * in this state for domestic consumption’’, as provided 
in Section 115-1102, and as provided in the following Section, 
as amended, ‘‘on gasoline used at any municipal * * * air 
field’’? 

We understand that it is the taxation of the use of gasoline 
in the sense of its consumption as a fuel that is forvidden in 
the Helson case, for, referring thereto, it is said in the Wallace 
case that the tax forbidden therein was ‘‘levied upon the use 
of gasoline ir. generating motive power for a ferry boat used 
exclusively ia interstate commerce’’. 

The word -‘used’’ admits of different meanings, such as 
sold, consumed in generating motive power, and stored and 
withdrawn. 

‘*A sale is one of the uses to which property can be sub- 
jected. As relating to one’s possession, the word ‘use’ in- 
cludes every purpose for which such property may be used’’, 
Gulf Refining Co. v. Smith, et al., 164 Ga. 811, 814, 139 S. E. 
716, 718. 


FUSION 


pt ES ee 
ie eee eae 


hs EES ESA RSS EER 


Cn os SN ASE NG IODIDE A TIT SAR ENP GY BEN a AD 


a 


Gasoline used in the state for domestic consumption, used 
at an air field, or used tor domestic consumption at an air 
field, is not gasoline used in generating motive power. Since 
the law qualifies use for domestic consumption, this is the use 
that is the basis of the tax, and not that of generating motive 
power in interstate commerce. 

Respondent stores and withdraws the gasoline on which 
petitioners collect the tax. Respondent uses the gasoline at 
the municipal air field, that is, it stores and withdraws the 
gasoline at the air field. Petitioners are not taxing the use 
of gasoline in generating motive power, after it is withdrawn 
from respondent’s storage tanks. If they were, they would 
tax all gasoline so used from the time respondent’s airplanes 
crossed the state line, as was attempted in the Helson case. 

However, we a. not think that the mere wording of the 
statute is controlling, for it has been repeatedly held by this 
Court, in passing on the legality of a tax, that it regards the 
substance and not the form. 

‘‘In maintaining rights asserted under the Federal Consti- 
tution, the decision of this Court is not dependent upon the 
form of a taxing scheme, or upon the characterization of it 
by the state court. We regard the substance rather than the 
form, and the controlling test is found in the operation and 
effect of the statute as applied and enforced by the State.’’ 
Gregg Dyeing Co. v. Query, 286 U. S. 472, 476. And at page 
478 it is said, ‘‘As to the first question (imposing a direct 
burden upon interstate commerce), we are not concerned with 
what the tax is called, but with what the statute does.’’ 

‘*The name by which the tax is described in the statute is, 
of course, immaterial. Its character must be determined by 
its incidents;’’ Dawson yv. Kentucky Distilleries, 255 U. S. 
288, 292. 

‘*On the other hand, if what is done is to reach the property 
and not to tax the gross earnings, the latter being taken mere- 
ly as an index or measure of the value of the former, it well 
may be that the objection urged against the tax is untenable; 
for, as this court has said, ‘by whatever name the tax or taxes 
may be called that are fixed by reference to the value of the 
property, if they are not imposed because of its use in inter- 
state or foreign commerce, and if they amount to no more 
than would be legitimate as an ordinary tax upon the prop- 
erty, valued with reference to the use in which it is employed, 


8 AR RN abe STAI ities 1] 


nelle 


they are not open to attack’ as restraining or burdening such © 
commerce.’’ Cudahy Packing Co. v. Minnesota, 246 U. S. 450, 
453. 

‘And, further, in determining whether a tax has such a ~ 
direct relation to interstate commerce as to be an exercise of | 
power prohibited by the commerce clause, our decision must 
regard the substance of the exaction—its operation and effect 
as enforced—and cannot depend upon the manner in which 
the taxing scheme has been characterized.’’ Kansas City Ry. 
v. Kansas, 240 U. S. 227, 231. 

‘“‘The difficulty has been, and is, to distinguish between 
legitimate attempts to exert the taxing power of the State 
and those laws which, though in the guise of taxation, impose 
real burdens upon interstate commerce as such.’’ U. 5. Ex- 
press Co. v. Minnesota, 223 U.S. 335, 344. 

‘‘Neither the state courts nor the legislatures, by giving 
the tax a particular name or by the use of some form of words, 
can take away our duty to consider its nature and effect. If it 
bears upon commerce among the states so directly as to 
amount to a regulation in a relatively immediate way, it will 
not be saved by name or form.’’ Galveston, Harrisbury ete. 
Ry. Co. v. Texas, 210 U. 8. 217, 227. 

‘‘Taxation is eminently practical, and is in fact brvvght to 
every man’s door, and for the purpose of deciding upon its 
validity a tax should be regarded in its actual, practical re- 
sults, rather than with reference to those theoretical or ab- 
stract ideas whose correctness is the subject of dispute and 
contradiction among those who are experts in the science of 
political economy.’’ Nicol v. Ames, 173 U.S. 509, 516. 

The most unfavorable view that can be taken of the Wyo- 
ming law is that it provides for the payment of a non-discrim- 
inatory license tax of four cents per gallon on all gasoline 
which has become a part of the general mass of the property 
of the State. Without ourselves presuming to define an excise 
tax, it has been frequently referred to by this Court as an 
indirect tax. We think that one of its characteristics is that 
only those are required to pay it who voluntarily acquire or 
use the article on which it is levied. That is, it is a tax levied 
on certain articles, such as tobacco, intoxicating liquors and 
gasoline. As was said by this Court in Patton v. Brady, Ex- 
ecutrix, 184 U. S. 608, 618-619: 


aS eeusee ort 
. 
¥ 
¥ 


PS aie. 
a A 


ms 


_ a 


‘‘Tt is true other counsel in their brief have advanced a very 
elaborate and ingenious argument to show that this is a direct 


» tax upon property which must be apportioned according to 


population within the rule laid down in the Income Taz cases, 
but, as we have seen, it is not a tax upon property as such but 
upon certain kinds of property, having reference to their 
origin and intended use.’’ 

The District Judge sustained the tax as compensation to the 
cities for the facilities furnished and their outlay in main- 
tenance and upkeep of the air fields. We do not deem it nec- 


_ essary to cite additional authorities to those relied on by him. 
_ He having found the compensation reasonable, we think this 
_ finding will be accepted by this Court. (R. 126-131) 


a oe 


Conclusion 


It is uniformly held that the user of the article pays the 
excise tax. In enforcing the payment of the gasoline tax by 
respondent, petitioners are collecting a uniform, non-discrim- 
inatory tax on all users of gasoline in the State, and on which 
no tax has been paid. To declare the tax invalid, as admin- 
istered against respondent, is to place respondent in a pre- 
ferred class as a user of gasoline in generating motive power 
in interstate commerce. 

We respectfully submit that the decree of the United States 
Circuit Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit, remanding 
this cause with instructions to issue the injunction, should be 
reversed, with directions to affirm in all respects the decree of 
dismissal rendered by the United States District Court for 
the District of Wyoming. 

Respectfully submitted, 


James A. GreENWooD, 
Attorney General of the State of 
Wyoming. 


Ricuarp J. Jackson, 
Deputy Attorney General of the 
State of Wyoming.

THE STATUTES OF WYOMING LEVY A LICENSE TAX OF 
FOUR CENTS PER GALLON ON ALL GASOLINE USED 
OR SOLD FOR DOMESTIC CONSUMPTION, AFTER ITS 
STORAGE IN THE STATE, AND DO NOT TAX THE USE 
OF GASOLINE AS A MEANS BY WHICH INTERSTATE

If. 
JURISDICTION. 


By reason of Section 240 (a), Aet of February 
13th, 1925, ch. 229; 483 Statutes at Large 936 (U.S. 
C., Title 28, Sec. 347 a) this Court has jurisdiction 
to grant the petition for writ of certiorari. How- 
ever, as hereinafter pointed out, the reasons usually 
required by this Court for the issuance of a writ of 
eertiorart: are entirely lacking in this case (Rule 38 


of this Court). 


III 
STATEMENT OF THE CASE. 


As a statement of the case petitioners content 
themselves with a quotation from the opinion of the 
Cireuit Court of Appeals. The summary of facts 
in this quotation in some material respects includes 
deductions by the writer of the opinion which are 
not supported by the Reeord and are not coneurred 
in by the other members of the Court. Respondent 
therefore deems it advisable io make a complete 


statement of the case as disclosed by the Record. 


In this case respondent sought to enjoin the 
State Treasurer of Wyoming from exacting payment 
of a tax imposed under the laws of Wyoming with 
respect to gasoline used by the airplanes of respond- 


ent While engaged in interstate commerce and to re- 


5 


cover certain sums paid as taxes, under protest (R. 
11-12, 6, 45). 


Respondent is a corporation organized under the 
laws of the State of Washington and qualified to 
do business in the State of Wyoming (R. 2,61). The 
petitioner William H. Edelman was State Treasurer 
of Wyoming at the time of the institution of the 
action (R.31). The petitioner Harry R. Weston was 
State Treasurer of Wyoming at the time the case 
came on for trial in the District Court (R. 47). Both 
Edelman and Weston are residents of the State of 
Wyoming (R. 47). The petitioners, the City of 
Cheyenne and the City of Rock Springs, are munic- 
ipal corporations organized under the laws of the 
State of Wyoming and, as such, are residents of that 
State (R. 47, 48). 


Respondent is engaged in the transportation by 
air of mail, passengers and express, between the 
cities of Chicago, Illinois and San Francisco. Cali- 
fornia (R. 89). Mail is carried under contract with 
the United States Government; express and passen- 
gers are carried for hire (R. 63, 89). Gasoline is 
used as fuel to propel the airplanes of respondent 
(R. 89). 


The planes of the respondent land in Wyoming 
at the Cities of Cheyenne and Rock Springs (R. 101), 


The first stop to the east of Cheyenne is North Platte, 


Neeser 


ay 
6 


Nebraska, and the first stop to the west of Rock 
Springs is Salt Lake City, Utah (R. 101). The 
amount of intrastate commerce, as compared with 
the interstate commerce in Wyoming, is insignificant, 
and the interestate and the intrastate transportation 
of mail, passengers and express are so commingled 
that it would be impossible to apportion the tax be- 
tween them (R. 94-95, 49). 


The gasoline that is placed in the respondent’s 
planes at Cheyenne and Rock Springs, Wyoming, is 
stored in the tanks of the respondent at the Cheyenne 
and Rock Springs airports and at the time it is taken 
from the tanks for use, a record is made of the amount 
used in interstate commerce and the amount used 
locally (R. 97-98). The tax is collected by the State 
Treasurer after the gasoline is used by the respond- 
ent (R. 97). 


The taxes on gasoline which is used locally are 
paid without protest (R. 103). The taxes on gasoline 
used in respondent’s planes as the fuel to propel the 
planes in interstate commerce are paid under pro- 
test, and these are the taxes which are the subject of 
this action (R. 93, 103). The protested taxes are paid 
in order to avoid the heavy penalties imposed by the 


Wyoming laws for non-payment (R. 90-91). 


The taxes involved in this action were imposed 


by the State of Wyoming with respect to gasoline 


a 
7 


placed in the planes of respondent in the State of 
Wyoming between March 15, 1930, and May 12, 1931, 
for use in interstate commerce (R. 55-56). The taxes 
paid under protest by respondent to the State of 
Wyoming during this period were in excess of 
$15,000.00 CR. 55-56). 


The tax in question is imposed by ch. 115, Art. 
11, Wyoming Revised Statutes for 1931, a copy of 


which is set forth in the appendix to petitioner’s brief : 


on file herein. 


eae FRR AT 


The gasoline with respect to which the tax in 
question was imposed was purchased by respondent 
hoth within and without the State of Wyoming (R. 
97). On or before the 15th day of each month, the 
respondent paid to the State Treasurer the tax on all 
gasoline consumed by the respondent during the pre- 


ecding calendar month (R. 99), 


The District Court held that the tax in question, 
irrespective of whether the gasoline was acquired 
Within or without the State of Wyoming, was a tax 
upon the ase of gasoline. The Court recognized that, 
under the decision of this Court in Helson v. Ken- 
lucky, 279 U.S. 45, a tax imposed upon the use of 
gasoline in interstate commerce is an unlawful bur- 
den upon that commerce. The District Court held, 
however, that inasmuch as the proceeds of the tax 


were turned over by the State to the Cities of Chey- 


¥ 
Pm 
* | 


SRE ERIE RIE OES TARAS TO OEM <a 


enne and Rock Springs for the maintenance and 
improvement of their airports which were used by 
the respondent, the tax was in effect an exaction 
of compensation for the use of these airports and 
consequently was not an unlawful burden upon inter- 
state commerce (R. 118-131). 


The Circuit Court of Appeals held that the tax 
in question was a license tax on the wholesaler of 
gasoline which required the wholesaler to pay a tax 
upon the sale or use of all gasoline sold or used by 
him in Wyoming; that respondent was required to 
pay the tax with respect to gasoline purchased by 
respondent from a wholesaler in Wyoming but that 
the tax did not apply with respect to gasoline pur- 
chased by the respondent outside of the State of Wyo- 
ming (R. 134-144). 


IV. 
SUMMARY OF ARGUMENT. 


I. Petitioners’ contention that the Cireuit Court 
of Appeals held the tax in question invalid as to 
gasoline purchased outside of the State is erroneous. 


The Cirenit Court of Appeals took the view that the 


3 Pat et ReaasRi aoe SERRATE sata Ut sabia eit ae 8 ont cain pis SgWse ams PF ey abies ip eR ee tte teen eee Ee eS 


Wyoming Act merely imposed a license tax upon 
persons engaged in selling gasoline as wholesalers in 
the State and that therefore no tax could be collected 
under the statute in question as to gasoline purchased 


by respondent outside of Wyoming. 


8 EMMA SDS fie ROLES EIEN 


II. If petitioners are correct in their conten- 
tion that the Cireuit Court of Appeals held the Wyo- 
ming tax invalid with respect to gasoline purchased 
hy respondent outside of Wyoming, the decision of 
the Cireuit Court is in full aecord with the decisions 
of this Court. 


V. 
ARGUMENT. 


Port I. 


PETITIONERS’ CONTENTION THAT THE Circuit CouRT 
or APPEALS HELD THE WYOMING GASOLINE Tax AcT 
InvaLiy With Respect TO GASOLINE PURCHASED 
OUTSIDE OF THE STATE Is ERRONEOUS. 


On the second page of petitioners’ brief (p. 8 of 
petition and brief) the petitioners make the follow- 
ing statement: 


‘ 


“The opinion of the United States Circuit 
Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit sustains 
the tax as legal on all such gasoline procured 
by respondent on purchases within the State of 
Wyoming and holds the tax to be illegal on all 
such gasoline procured by respondent on pur- 
chases outside the State of Wyoming and then 
brought into said state and used in propelling 
its airplanes in interstate commerce.”’ 


As we read the opinion of the Circuit Court of 


Appeals, that Court did not attempt to pass upon 


ae — 


i 


10 


the validity of the Wyoming Gasoline Tax Act as ap- 
plied to gasoline purchased by respondent outside of 
the State. The Circuit Court simply held that the Wyo- 
ming Act did not apply with respect to such gaso- 
line. Respondent does not agree with the Circuit 
Court in its conclusions as to the nature of the tax 
imposed. Respondent has consistently taken the posi- 
tion throughout this case that the tax imposed upon 
respondent with respect to gasoline purchased both 
within and without Wyoming is a tax upon the use of 
gasoline. The Cireuit Court of Appeals, however, 
construes the Wyoming Act as imposing a_ license 
tax upon persons engaged in selling gasoline as whole- 
salers in Wyoming. <As to gasoline purchased by the 
respondent from a wholesaler in Wyoming, the Court 
said that the wholesaler was obligated to pay the 
tax and that when respondent itself paid the tax it 
was simply discharging the primary obligation of the 
wholesaler. The Court sets forth ifs view in this con- 
nection in the following language: 

“The tax here under consideration is the li- 
cense tax against the wholesaler exacted of him 
for the privilege of carrying on the business of 
selling gasoline, and when appellant made its 
purchase it had an option under the statuie of 
then paying the tax added to the purchase price, 
or of assuming its payment and accounting to 
the State Treasurer therefor after it had used 
the gasoline. It chose to do the latter and thus 


LiL SRA PTASE LIONS AEN SG LATED MAI RIPE BILL PANEER LENDING PUNT OP 


11 


discharged a primary obligation of the whole- 
saler.’’ 


The view of the character of the tax imposed by 
the Wyoming Statute (thus expressed by the Cir- 
cuit Court of Appeals) necessarily excluded from the 
operation of the statute the gasoline purchased by 
the respondent outside the State of Wyoming. The 
Circuit Court of Appeals has so held, and directed 
that an injunction issue against the collection of any 
tax with respect to the gasoline purchased by the 
respondent outside the State of Wyoming for use in 
fueling its planes in interstate commerce. The fact 
that the injunction is limited to gasoline used in inter- 
state commerce does not militate against the view 
that the Cireuit Court of Appeals held the tax inap- 
plicable to gasoline purchased outside of the State 
rather than invalid as applied to gasoline used in 
interstate commerce, The limitation with respect 
to the injunction is apparently derived from the 
nature of the issues originally presented by the plead- 
ings and the respondent’s brief on appeal to the Cir- 


cenit Court of Appeals. 


If respondent is correct in its interpretation of 
the decision of the Circuit Court of Appeals it is 
clear that the usual reasons for granting a petition 
for a writ of certiorvart, as outlined in Rule 38 of this 
Court, do not exist in this case. The decision of the 


Circuit Court of Appeals is not in conflict with the 


12 


decisions of other Cireuit Courts or of the Courts of 
the State of Wyoming. The decision is not in eon- 
flict with the weight of authority and no important 
question of Federal law is involved in the decision. 
The decision rests solely upon the construction placed 
upon the Wyoming Gasoline Tax Act by the Circuit 
Court. It therefore appears that no sufficient reason 


exists for a review of the case by this Court. 


Forsyth v. Hammond, 166 U.S. 506. 


Point LI. 


Ir Prririoners ARE Correct IN THEIR CONTENTION 
THAT THE Cincurr Court or AppEALS Dip Houp 
INVALID THE TAX IMPOSED BY THE Wyoming Gaso- 
LINE Tax Act ON GASOLINE PURCHASED BY RE- 
SPONDENT OUTSIDE OF THE STATE OF WYOMING AND 
THEREAFTER IMPORTED INTO SAtp STATE FOR USE IN 
Ivs AIRPLANES IN INTERSTATE COMMERCE, SUCH 
Decisions Is ix Fun Accorp Wirt tHe Decisions 
or THIs Courr. 

The petitioners’ position as to the nature of the 
tax is not clear, aithough petitioners recognize that 
it isan excise tax. (See page 8 of petition and brief.) 
The Wyoming Act is similar to many state gasoline 
tax statutes in that it imposes a tax upon the sale or 
use of gasoline. If the Wyoming Gasoline Tax Act 
applies to gasoline purchased by respondent outside 


of the State of Wyoming it must attach to the use of 


VP Deefn O TT 


#4 


13 


that gasoline. Inasmuch as the gasoline was pur- 
chased by respondent outside the jurisdiction of the 
State, there is no act to which the tax may attach 


other than the use of the gasoline. 


Petitioners contend that inasmuch as the gaso- 
line purchased by respondent outside of Wyoming 
came to rest in Wyoming before it was consumed 
hy respondent’s planes, such gasoline was subject to 
the tax in question. Petitioners state that the tax 
is levied on gasoline which has acquired ‘‘a fixed situs 
within the State of Wyoming.’’ This statement is 
not supported by the language of the statutes. The 
tax is not based upon the situs of the gasoline but 


upon its sale or use. 


Respondent agrees that a State may validly im- 
pose a property tax upon property within its bor- 
ders, even though that property is used in interstate 
commerce, The respondent does in fact pay prop- 
erty taxes to the State of Wyoming on gasoline held 
hy it in that State for use in interstate commerce 
(R, 105). Furthermore, a State may impose excise 
taxes on purely intrastate transactions whieh are only 
remotely connected with interstate commerce. (See 
Kastern Air Transport, Ince. v. South Carolina Tas 
Commission et al, 285 U.S. 147.) But in the instant 
case the particular act that is taxed is the use of gaso- 


line —and that use is in interstate commerce. 


4 


rath 


2p NR STS 


Theta IO RG CR ER OS 


Bice PIS ty A Lia eS Sicadison Shoes: 


14 


The tax being imposed upon the use of gasoline, 
the fact that the gasoline came to rest within the 
State of Wyoming is unimportant. Irrespective of 
where the gasoline was situated at the time it was 
placed in the planes of respondent, the tax did not 
attach until that gasoline was actually used. Inas- 
much as the use of that gasoline was in interstate 
commerce, the tax constituted an unlawful burden 
upon that commerce, under the decisions of this 
Court. 


In the case of Helson v. Kentucky, 279 U.S. 245, 
decided by this Court in 1929, the plaintiffs in error 
operated a ferry boat on the Ohio River between 
Kentucky and Illinois, doing an exclusively inter- 
state business. The gasoline which was used to oper- 
ate the ferry boat was purchased in the State of 
Illinois, but about seventy-five per cent of it was 
consumed in the State of Kentucky. Under the Ken- 
tucky Jaw a tax was imposed upon the sale or use 
of gasoline within the borders of the State. This 
Court, in holding that the imposition of this tax upon 
the use of gasoline within the State of Kentucky con- 
stituted an unlawful burden upon interstate com- 


meree, said: 


“The tax is exacted as the price of the privi- 
lege of using an instrumentality of interstate 
commerce. It reasonably cannot be distinguished 
from a tax for using a locomotive or a car em- 


15 


ployed in such commerce. A tax laid upon the 
use of the ferryboat would present an exact par- 
allel. And is not the fuel consumed in propelling 
the boat an instrumentality of commerce no less 
than the boat itself? A tax which falls directly 
upon the use of one of the means by which com- 
merce is carried on directly burdens that com- 
merce. If a tax cannot be laid by a state upon 
the interstate transportation of the subjects of 
commerce, as this Court definitely has held, it 
is little more than repetition to say that such a 


tax cannot be laid upon the use of a medium by 
which such transportation is effected. ‘All re- 
traints by exactions in the form of taxes upon 
such transportation or upon acts necessary to its 
completion, are so many invasions of the exelu- 
sive power of Congress to regulate that portion 
of commerce between the states.’ Gloucester 

Ferry Co. v. Pennsylvania, 114 U. S. 214, 29 L. 

Kd. 165, 1 Inters. Com. Rep. 382, 5 Sup. Ct. Rep. 

826.”’ 

Petitioners, in their brief, have referred to the 
case of HRastern Air Transport, Ine., v. South Caro- 
lina Tar Commission et al., 285 U.S. 147. In that 
case this Court sustained a tax on the sale of gaso- 
line, although the gasoline was thereafter used in 
propelling airplanes in interstate commerce. It was 
held that the sale was purely a local transaction and 
the effect of the tax on such commerce was no greater 


vr different than a general property tax upon the 


16 


same property would be. This Court recognized, how- 
ever, that a general property tax or a tax upon purely 
local sales is manifestly different from a tax ‘‘laid 
directly upon the use of gasoline in interstate trans- 
portation.’? In the Eastern Air Transport case this 
Court recognized and approved the rule laid down in 
Helson v. Kentucky that a tax imposed upon the use 
of gasoline, when that use is in interstate commerce, 


constitutes an unlawful burden upon that commerce. 


The petitioners also refer to the case of Gregg 
Dyeing Co. v. Query, 286 U.S. 472. In this ease a 
tax Was sustained upon the use of gasoline purchased 
outside of the State and imported for use in a elean- 
ing business earried on solely within the State. LHow- 
ever, this Court recognized that a tax imposed upon 
the use of gasoline, in interstate commerce is invalid, 
saying: 

‘There is an exception in the case of a tax 
directly on use in interstate commerce as on use 
in interstate transportation. /7elson v. Keutueky, 
279 U. S. 245, 252; Kastern Air Transport v. 
South Carolina Tar Commission, decided Mareh 
14, 1932.”’ 


It cannot be questioned that under the decisions 
of this Court a tax imposed upon the use of gasoline 
in interstate commerce constitutes a violation of the 
commerce clause of the Federal constitution. If the 


petitioners are correct in their contention that the 


Reb NIE ANE OEIC: BT ae 


7 


Cireuit Court of Appeals did hold the Wyoming Gaso- 
line Tax unconstitutional with respect to gasoline pur- 
chased outside of the State, then it must be said that 
that Court was following the decisions of this Court. 
As we have heretofore stated, it is our opinion that 
the Circuit Court of Appeals does not rest its decision 
upon any constitutional ground. However, if the 
Circuit Court of Appeals did intend to pass upon 
the constitutionality of the Wyoming Gasoline Tax 
Act, as applied to gasoline purchased outside of the 
State, it was clearly correct in reaching the result 
that it did. If the Wyoming Gasoline Tax Act does 
apply to gasoline purchased outside of the State it 
must attach to the use of that gasoline and, under 
the decisions of this Court heretofore referred to, the 
tux imposed upon the use of gasoline in interstate 


commerce is clearly invalid. 


Respectfully submitted,
