THE FACT SITUATION AND THE QUESTION 
INVOLVED. 


Respondent, BOEING AIR TRANSPORT, INC., 
is engaged in transportation in interstate commerce 
by airplane (R. 89). It uses two municipally-owned 
airports in the State of Wyoming (R. 68-100). Gaso- 
line is the fuel used for propelling the airplanes (R. 
89, 102). Boeing Air Transport, Inec., purchases its 
gasoline both inside and outside the State of Wyoming 
and maintains storage facilities at the airports (R. 97). 
A small amount of local transportation business is car- 
ried on, but the gasoline used in the local business ean- 
not be separated from that used in the interstate com- 
merce business (R. 89, 94, 95, 102). A small amount 
of gasoline is sold by Boeing Air Transport, Inc., at 
the airports to other users (R. 90, 100). Some gaso- 
line is used for testing and washing motors and for 
automobiles (R. 93). The balance of the gasoline is 
used for flights from the airports at Cheyenne and 
Rock Springs, in Wyoming, to points outside that State 
(R. 93, 103). The gasoline so used is the basis of the 
controversy before this Court (R. 5-6, 44, 93). 


The question involved on this review of the judg- 
ment of the Cireuit Court of Appeals for the Tenth 
Circuit is simply this: Can the respondent Boeing 
Air Transport, Inec., be required to pay to the State 


of Wyoming, under the present statute, a tax meas-

amendments to the original Act. For this reason we

Wyoming Gasoline Tax Act did not apply with respect 
to such gasoline. Petitioners in their supplemental

brief in opposition to the petition wherein this con-

SUMMARY OF ARGUMENT. 


I. The recent decision of this Court in the ease of 
The Nashville-Chattanooga d& St. Louis Ry. Co. v. 
Wallace, No. 176, October Term, 1932, decided Febru- 
ary 6, 1933, does not govern the case at bar. 


II. The use which is the subject of the Wyoming 
Gasoline Tax is not storage or withdrawal from stor- 
age, but actual consumption in operation of respond- 
ent’s planes in interstate commerce, and, under the 
decision of Helson v. Kentucky, 279 U.S. 245, a tax 
upon such use constitutes an unlawful burden upon 


that commerce. 


III. The Act under which this tax is imposed is 
purely a revenue measure and its imposition upon the 
use of gasoline by the respondent’s planes in interstate 
commerce is in no sense an exaction of compensation 
for the use of the Cheyenne and Rock Springs airports. 


IV. If the tax imposed on gasoline taken into 


respondent’s planes on the Cheyenne airport for use 


"eens 


7 


in interstate commerce is construed as an exaction of 
compensation for the use of the Cheyenne airport, 
it is in violation of Section 10, Article I, of the Con- 
stitution of the United States because it impairs the 
obligation of that contract between the respondent 
and the City of Cheyenne dated July 1, 1929, and en- 
tered into prior to the effective date of the statute 


under which the tax is imposed. 


ARGUMENT. 


A 
THe Recent Decision or THis Court in THE Case OF 
Tur NaAsHVILLE-CHaATraNnooGa & Sr. Louis Ry. Co. 
v. WaLiace, No. 176, OcropeR TerRM, 1932, De- 
cCIpED FEBRUARY 6, 1933, Dors Nor Govern THE 
Case aT Bar. 

In petitioner’s supplemental brief great reliance 
is placed upon The Nashville-Chattanooga & St. Louis 
Ry. Co.v. Roy C. Wallace, Comptroller, et al., No. 176, 
a decision of this Court announced February 6, 1933. 
In this ease a tax imposed upon the storage and with- 
drawal from storage of gasoline was upheld, although 
the gasoline, after its withdrawal from storage, was 
used in interstate commerce. A sharp distinction was 
drawn between a tax imposed upon a transaction c«om- 
pleted prior to the use of gasoline in interstate com- 
meree, and a tax upon the use of the gasoline. It was 
held that since the storage and withdrawal from stor- 


age of gasoline was completed prior to its use in inter- 


LAA PDE ION Ay BBA et Mh 


8 


state commerce, the tax did not impose an unlawful 
burden upon that commerce. This Court said: 


“The power to tax property, the sum of all 
the rights and powers incident to ownership, neces- 
sarily includes the power to tax its constituent ele- 
ments. See Bromley v. McCaughn, 280 U.S. 124, 
136-138. Hence, there can be no valid objection to 
the taxation of the exercise of any right or power 
incident to appellant’s ownership of the gasoline, 
which falls short of a tax directly imposed on 
its use in interstate commerce, deemed forbidden 
in Helson v. Kentucky, supra. Here the tax is im- 
posed on the successive exercise of two of those 
powers, the storage and withdrawal from storage 
of the gasoline. Both powers are completely exer- 
cised before use of the gasoline in interstate com- 
merce begins. The tax imposed upon their exercise 
is therefore not one imposed on the use of the 
gasoline as an instrument of commerce and the 
burden of it is too indirect and remote from thie 
function of interstate commerce itself to trans- 
gress constitutional limitations. See Eastern Air 


7 99 


Transport v. Tar Commission, 285 U.S. 147. 


The doctrine of Helson v. Kentucky, 279 U.S. 
245, that a tax upon the use of gasoline in interstate 
commerce is an unlawful burden upon that commerce 


was again approved by this Court, as follows: 


‘*‘We cannot say that the tax is a forbidden 
burden on interstate commerce because appellant 
uses the gasoline, subsequent to the incidence of 
the tax, as an instrument of interstate commerce. | 
Taxes said to burden interstate commerce directly | 
when levied upon or measured by the operation 


SR ia he ig aA 0 ON SOME I RB A LCLOE LEO 


9 


of interstate commerce or gross receipts derived 

from it, are beyond the State taxing power, East 

Ohio Gas Co. v. Tax Commission, 283 U. 8. 465, 

470; Sprout v. South Bend, 277 U.8. 163, 170, 171; 

Crew Levick Co. v. Pennsylvania, 245 U. 8S. 292, 

297, and a tax levied upon the use of gasoline in 

generating motive power for a ferry boat used 

exclusively in interstate commerce has been held 
to be so direct and immediate a burden on the 
commerce itself as to be invalid. Helson v. Ken- 

tucky, 279 U.S. 245." 

Petitioners’ argument, in their supplemental brief, 
resolves itself into this proposition: that inasmuch 
as the gasoline aequired by respondent outside of 
Wyoming and brought into that State for use could 
lawfully have been subjected to a tax under a statute 
similar to the Tennessee Act involved in the Wallace 
ease, therefore the tax in the instant ease is valid. The 
petitioners cite cases in support of the proposition 
that this Court will ‘‘regard the substance, rather than 
the form, and the controlling test is found in the opera- 
tion and effect of the statute as applied and enforced 
by the State.”’ We are quite in aecord with the peti- 
tioners in their desire to have this Court regard the 
substance of this Act rather than the form. How- 
ever, petitioners are in effeet asking this Court to 
amend the statute and impose a tax upon the storage 


of gasoline instead of upon its use.

Il.

The petitioners in their supplemental brief for 
the first time make the point that the subject of the 
tax in question is the storage and withdrawing from 
storage by the respondent of the gasoline used by it. 


On page 4 of their supplemental brief the petition- 
ers say: 

‘*Respondent stores and withdraws the gaso- 
line on which petitioners collect the tax. Respond- 
ent uses the gasoline at the municipal air field; 
that is, it stores and withdraws the gasoline at the 
air field. Petitioners are not taxing the use of 
gasoline in generating motive power after it is 
withdrawn from respondent’s storage tanks. If 
they were they would tax all gasoline so used from 
the time respondent’s airplanes crossed the State 
line, as was attempted in the Helson ease.’’ (Italics 
are ours. ) 


It is apparent from this quotation that the peti- 
tioners base their present position upon the facts alone 
that the respondent stores at the airports the gasoline 
which it uses, and likewise withdraws the gasoline 


which operates its planes in interstate commerce from 


that storage. The undisputed evidence as to the nature 
of the tax and the method of its collection shows clearly 


11 


ae 
*: 
oa 
% 
& 
# 


the unsoundness of petitioners’ argument. On cross- 
examination, Mr. D. B. Colyer testified (R. 100), as 
follows: 

“Our method of making the payments is as 
directed by the State. For illustration, during the 
month of June we paid the taxes on gasoline used 
during May, and these were to be paid before the 
15th of the month subsequent to that in which the 
gasoline was used. None of the tax is paid in ad- 


ranee of the use. It is all paid on the 15th of the 
month after it is used.” 


The fact that petitioners do not attempt to tax gaso- 
line in the tanks of respondent’s planes as they cross 
the Wyoming boundary lends no color to petitioners’ 
contention. It would be highly impracticable, if not 
impossible, to impose a tax on such gasoline. The fail- 
ure by the petitioners to do the impossible is hardly 
a proper aid to a construction of the statute in ques- 


tion. 


The argument now advanced on behalf of petition- 
ers does, however, serve the purpose of bringing 
sharply to the attention of the Court the crux of the 
controversy involved in this ease with respect to the 
gasoline which is imported into the State of Wyoming 
hy the respondent and used by the respondent ex- 
clusively in propelling its airplanes in interstate com- 


meree, In order to sustain the tax imposed by the 


12 


Wyoming statute upon such gasoline, it is necessary 
for the petitioners to argue that the word ‘‘use’’ and 
the words ‘‘used for domestic consumption,’’ as em- 
ployed in the statute, mean, with respect to respondent, 
the storage of gasoline and its withdrawal from stor- 
age within the State. 


The words ‘‘use’’? and ‘used for domestie con- 
sumption,’’ as employed in paragraphs 2 and 3 of the 
Wyoming statute, are apparently so employed in their 
ordinary sense. The word ‘‘use’’ alone implies an ap- 
plication of the thing itself, or, as stated by Webster, 
**the act of employing anything or applving it to one’s 
service; the state of being so employed or applied.” 
This meaning of the word ‘‘use’’ was accepted and 
applied by this Court to a statute imposing a tax 
‘‘upon the use of a foreign-built vacht’’ in the case of 
Billings v. U. S., 232 U.S. 261. The word ‘‘consump- 
tion’’ qualifies the word ‘‘use’’ in paragraph 2 of the 
statute, wherein it is provided that there is levied ‘‘a 
license tax * * * on all gasoline used or sold in this 
State for domestic consumption.’” The statute clearly 
states that the use of gasoline which is subject to the 
tax is a use for domestic consumption. The retention 
of gasoline in storage for some future disposition, or 
the mere withdrawal from storage does not result in 
the ‘‘consumption’’ of the gasoline. It is well-known 
that the gasoline tax was designed to compel the own- 


ers of motor vehicles to contribute to the construction 


SET SN ONE 


13 


and maintenance of highways. A motor vehicle uses 
gasoline by consuming it. It is absurd to contend that 
the Wyoming legislature intended to impose a tax 
upon the storage or withdrawal from storage of gaso- 
line by a statute which taxes ‘‘gasoline used for domes- 


tic consumption.”’ 


The significance of the word ‘‘use,’’ as employed 
in the Wyoming statute, is made plain by subdivision 
2 of Section 1, defining gasoline. This section provides 
that ‘‘gasoline is hereby defined as a volatile substance 
produced from petroleum * * * and such other volatile 
and inflammable liquids produced or compounded for 
the purpose of operating or propelling motor vehicles 
***?? This language indubitably indicates that at the 
time this legislation was enacted the legislature was 
thinking in terms of the operation and propulsion of 
vehicles by the generation of motive power through 
the use of gasoline. When, therefore, in the sueceed- 
ing paragraph of Section 2, the legislature imposed 
the tax on all gasoline used for domestic consumption, 
and in paragraph 3 provided for the payment of the 
tax by wholesalers upon all gasoline used by them, and 
upon every person who should use any gasoline in the 
State upon which the tax has not been paid by any 
wholesaler in the State, it meant by the word ‘‘use”’ 
such a use and such a use only as would necessarily 


result in the consumption or exhaustion of the gasoline, 


ttt 


14 


Respondent is unfamiliar with any reported de- 
cision which supports petitioners’ contention that the 
tax upon gasoline used, as imposed by the Wyoming 
statute, is not a tax upon the use of gasoline as used 
by respondent’s planes. In the cases of Bowman v. 
Continental Oil Co., 256 U. S. 642; Hart Refineries v. 
Harmon, 278 U. 8. 499, and Helson v. Kentucky, 279 
U.S. 245, it is clear that this Court viewed a tax upon 
the use of gasoline as a tax upon its consumption. 


The petitioners attempt to distinguish the instant 
ease from Helson v. Kentucky, supra, upon the ground 
that in the instant case the gasoline came to rest within 
the State of Wyoming before its use by respondent’s 
planes in interstate commerce. The mere fact that the 
gasoline came to rest in the taxing state prior to its 
use in interstate commerce is unimportant. In State 
Freight Tar Case, 15 Wall. 232, a State tax imposed 
upon freight taken up within the State and carried out 
of it was held invalid. The fact that the property on 
which the tax was imposed originated within the tax- 
ing State did not validate the tax. Likewise, in the 
instant case, the tax is not based upon the situs of the 
gasoline but upon its use and that use is in interstate 


commerce. 


Tt is true that in this Court’s recent decision in 
The Nashville C. & St. L. R. Co. v. Wallace, No. 176, 


decided February 6, 1933, it is said that where gasoline 


has come to rest in storage the State is free to tax it 


15 


as it would any other property in the State, notwith- 
standing its prospective use as an instrument of inter- 
state commerce. It is further said that the power to 
tax property, which means the taxation of the sum of 
all the rights and powers incident to ownership, neces- 
sarily includes also the power to tax its constituent 
elements. The Court, however, plainly says, in exactly 
the same connection, that these principles do not con- 
fer upon the State the right to tax property, or the 
elements of property, by a tax directly imposed upon 
the use of the property in interstate commerce. 


The tax imposed by the Tennessee Act involved 
in the ease of The Nashville, ete., Ry. Co. v. Wallace, 
supra, was expressly a privilege tax imposed upon 
persons and corporations ‘‘engaged in or carrying on 
the business * * * of selling or storing or distributing 
gasoline * * *.’’ Insofar as the tax was imposed upon 
persons engaged in storing gasoline, it was measured 
by the amount withdrawn from storage, whether such 
withdrawal was for sale or other use. Hence, the tax 
was distinctly imposed upon a privilege connected with 
the ownership of property, which in and of itself could 
be completely exercised before any use was made of 
the gasoline in interstate commerce; as could also the 
privilege of selling gasoline which was the subject of 
the tax in Eastern Air Transport v. South Carolina 
Tar Comm., 285 U. S. 147. But, as has been pointed 
out above, the Wyoming statute does not attempt t 


PETES DEON GIT IRS R 


ttre 


PRAT SY 


AOA TRIES TEES INTE —— 


16 


impose the tax upon the storage of gasoline or its 
withdrawal from storage. Except for the tax on gaso- 
line sold, it is only the use which is taxed, and this 
‘‘use,’? as applied to the gasoline placed by the re- 
spondent in its airplanes at the airports in the State 
of Wyoming for propelling said airplanes in carrying 
on respondent’s interstate commerce business, can 
mean only the use of such gasoline in interstate com- 
merce. The case at bar is therefore within the princi- 
ple of Helson v. Kentucky, 279 U.S. 245, and not with- 
in the principle of Easiern Air Transport v. South 
Carolina Tar Commission, 285 U.S. 147, or The Nash- 
ville, etc. Ry. Co. v. Wallace, decided February 6, 1933. 


ITT. 


THE Acr Unper Wuicuh Tuis Tax Is Imposep Is 
PurRELY A REVENUE MEASURE AND Its IMPpost- 
TION Upon THE USE OF GASOLINE BY THE RE- 
SPONDENT’S PLANES IN INTERSTATE COMMERCE Is 
IN No SENSE AN EXACTION OF COMPENSATION FOR 
THE USE OF THE CHEYENNE AND Rock SPRINGS 
ATRPORTS. 


In the supplemental brief of petitioners the fol- 


lowing statement appears: 


“The District Judge sustained the tax as com- 
pensation to the cities for the facilities furnished 
and their outlay in maintenance and upkeep of 
the air fields. We do not deem it necessary to 
cite additional authorities to those relied on by 
him. He having found the compensation reason-

able, we think this finding will be accepted by this 

Court (R. 126-131).”’ 

In their brief and argument before the Cireuit 
Court of Appeals petitioners practically abandoned 
their contention that the tax in question was in effect 
an exaction of compensation for the use of the Chey- 
enne and Rock Springs Airports. The Circuit Court 
of Appeals did not rest its decision upon any such 
ground. In petitioners’ petition for rehearing before 
the Cireuit Court of Appeals no mention was made of 
this contention. In their brief filed in this Court in 
support of their petition for writ of certiorari the 
point was not suggested. However, in view of the 
fact that petitioners have raised the point in their 
supplemental brief, respondent feels compelled to 
point out briefly to this Court the reasons why there is 
no merit in the contention. 

The Distriet Court held that since the proceeds 
of the tax on gasoline collected at any municipal 
field were returned by the State to the municipal- 
ity for the purpose of maintaining and improving 
the field, the tax was in effect an exaction of compensa- 
tion for the use of the field. For the reasons herein- 
after assigned, respondent submits that the tax was 
a true tax and in no sense an exaction of compensation 
for the use of the Cheyenne and Rock Springs air- 


ports.

A, Tue Power To Exact COMPENSATION FOR THE Use 
or MunicipaL Atrports Is IN THE MUNICIPALITY 
OWNING THE AIRPORT AND IS NoT IN THE STATE OF 
WYOMING. 

By Section 5 of Chapter 72 of the Session Laws 
of 1927, the State of Wyoming authorized and em- 
powered municipal corporations to acquire lands for 
airport purposes and to lease or let any such airport 
upon terms deemed satisfactory. Pursuant to this 
power conferred on municipalities, the cities of Chey- 
enne and Rock Springs aequired airports and there- 
after entered into leases with respondent. whereby, in 
consideration of certain fixed rentals am. other eoven- 
ants to be performed by the respomdent. the mon- 
exclusive use of the landing tields was granted to the 
respondent (R. 69, 80). The cities of Cheyenne and 
Rock Springs having acquired airports pursuant to 
legislative act, it would seem elear that only those cities 
could fix the compensation to be paid for the use of 
the airports. It is also clear that the legislature, havy- 
ing granted power to the cities to lease their airports 
upon terms deemed satisfactory, did not intend, by 
the gasoline tax act, to prescribe the compensation to 


be paid for the use of the airports. 


The power to exact compensation for the use of 
the Cheyenne and Rock Springs airports was in the 
cities owning those airports. The gasoline tax in ques- 


tion was exacted by the State of Wyoming. The cities, 


FESS ITT SCAN DRDO PVT rhe MER NN et alienate . aE 


PO GRR aR PE ROT ED 


19 


having acquired the airports, alone had the power to 
determine the compensation to be paid for their use. 
The conclusion must follow that the State of Wvyo- 
ming, in imposing the gasoline tax, was not seeking 
to exact compensation for the use of facilities entirely 
under the control of the cities of Cheyenne and Rock 


Springs. 


B. THERE Is No SUFFICIENT RELATION BETWEEN THE 
JFASOLINE TAX AND THE USE MADE OF THE AIRPORTS 
ro JusTiIFY HoLpInc THAT THE Tax Is A CHARGE 
MaprE MERELY AS COMPENSATION FOR Sucu UskE. 


This Court has, on several occasions, rejected the 
contention that a certain tax was an exaction of com- 
pensation for the use of a facility when it appeared 
that there was no necessary relationship between the 


payment of the tax and the use of the facility. 


The ease of Interstate Transit, Ine. v. Lindsey, 
283 U. 8. 183, is particularly applicable to the ease 
at bar. In this case there was involved a tax levied 
by the State of Tennessee upon concerns operating 
interstate motor buses upon the highways of the State, 
graduated according to carrving capacity. It was held 
that the tax was invalid because there was not a suffi- 
cient relationship between the measure employed and 
the extent or manner of use of the State highwavs 


such as would justify a holding that the tax was a 


ERAS PR VALE PUB LA ROMER AL TSP ADRES NEW ie Pe OT < Seale SRR 


S 


charge made merely as compensation for the use of 
the highways by interstate buses. This Court said: 


‘*But since a State may demand of one carry- 
ing on an interstate bus business only fair com- 
: pensation for what it gives, such imposition, al- 
though termed a tax, cannot be tested by standards 
which generally determine the validity of taxes. 
Being valid only if compensatory, the charge must 
be necessarily predicated upon the use made, or 
to be made, of the highways of the state. Clark v. 
Poor, 274 U.S. 554, supra. In the present act the 
amount of the tax is not dependent upon such use. 
It does not rise with an increase in mileage trav- 
elled, or even with the number of passengers ac- 
tually carried on the highways of the State. Nor 
is it related to the degree of wear and tear inci- 
dent to the use of motor vehicles of different sizes 
and weights, except insofar as this is indirectly 
affected by carrying capacity. The tax is propor- 
tioned solely to the earning capacity of the vehicle. 
Accordingly, there is here no sufficient relation 
between the measure employed and the erte t or 
manner of use, to justify holding that the tar 
was a charge made merely as compensation for the 
use of the highways by interstate buses. We need 
not therefore consider whether the tax exacted 
from this appellant is unreasonably large or un- 
justly discriminatory.”’ (Italies are curs.) 


it GARR Se seers 


As this Court clearly states in the foregoing case, 
in order to render valid a burden upon interstate com- 
meree as a charge for the use of a facility, it must ap- 


pear that the charge made is necessarily predicated 


upon the use of the facility. 


“ WSMhieho od Sa bn SSS ’ aeons ONT at os aadldiaeiee iene) 


21 


Although it may be urged that the more respond- 
ent uses the fields of Cheyenne and Rock Springs, the 
more gasoline it will use, and, consequently, the greater 
its tax, nevertheless such a statement can be nothing 
more than a rough approximation. There is no neces- 
sary connection between the amount of the tax and the 
amount of use of the airport by the person paving 


the tax. 


As an illustration, compare the use made of the 
Chevenne airport by the respondent with that by a 
company that does not engage in interstate commerce, 
but simply makes local flights from the airport. On its 
eastbound trips the respondent lands at Cheyenne and 
places sufficient gasoline in its tanks to carry the 
planes to at least its next stop, which is North Platte, 
Nebraska, and on its westbound trips the plane lands 
at Chevenne and sufficient gasoline is placed in its 
tanks to carry it to Rock Springs (R. 101). Obviously, 
the respondent’s planes will not land at the Cheyenne 
airport as often as they would if respondent did a sim- 
ilar amount of flying in a local business with practically 
all of its landings upon the Cheyenne field. It appears 
from the evidence that there is a company using the 
Cheyenne field engaged in a purely local business (R. 
100). Undoubtedly, this company pays much less in 
taxes in proportion to the amount that it uses the field 
than does respondent. Similar inequalities must nec- 


essarily result from the fact that the amount of the 


Bice: IESE Los ANE BES TE OBE CO BEALL DA AE TEI NA SOE TL Le LO Ul GN via on 


AL EIR a en Lg Me AO “oe ~~ 7 


tax is not at all dependent upon the amount of use that 


the taxpayer makes of the airport. 


It is clear from the foregoing that a tax or charge 
which is not in some reasonable manner measured by 
the use of the facility cannot be regarded as compensa- 
tion for such use. To this effect was the decision in 
the ease of Sprout v. South Bend, 277 U.S. 163, where- 
in this Court held invalid a license fee imposed by the 
City of South Bend upon motor buses using the city 
streets, the Court saying: 

‘*A flat tax, substantial in amount and _ the 
same for buses plying the streets continuously in 
local service and for buses making, as do many 
interstate buses, only a single trip daily, could 


hardly have been designed as a measure of the 
cost or value of the highways.”’ 


In the instant case the existence of leases between 
the Cities of Chevenne and Rock Springs and the re- 
spondent, wherein the respondent is granted the right 
to use the municipal airports of those cities upon the 
payment by respondent of certain rentals, is further 
proof that there is no sufficient relation between the 
tax and the use made of the airports by the respondent 
to justify a holding that the tax is a charge for the use 


of the airports. 


I> pen Sy Tl wee = Tz “+ oe SOI Ngee ea 


ay. 


Ir THE TaX IMPOSED ON GASOLINE TAKEN INTO ReE- 
SPONDENT’Ss PLANES ON THE CHEYENNE AIRPORT 
ror Use rx INTERSTATE CoMMERCE Is ConsTRUED 
AS AN EXACTION OF COMPENSATION FOR THE USE oF 
THE CHEYENNE AIRPORT, Ir Is IN VIOLATION OF 
Section 10, ARTICLE I, OF THE CONSTITUTION OF 
THE UniTep STATES Because It IMPAIRS THE OBLI- 
GATION OF THAT ConTRACT BETWEEN THE RESPOND- 
ENT AND THE City OF CHEYENNE Datep JULY 1, 
1929, aND ENTERED INTO PRIOR TO THE EFFECTIVE 
DaTE OF THE STATUTE UNDER WHICH THE Tax Is 
TMPOSED. 


Certain provisions of the lease between the City 
of Cheyenne and the respondent, appearing in full on 


pages 80-89 of the Record, are hereinafter summarized : 
(1) Term: 25 years, commencing July 1, 1929. 


(2) The Company agrees to pay an annual rental 
of $4050 for a certain hangar and space for 
the ereetion of an additional hangar. 


(3) Paragraph 14 provides: 


“The City agrees that the Boeing Company 
shall be entitled to the full use of said munici- 
pal fie'd and landing field during the term 
hereof on terms as favorable as may _ be 
granted by the City to any other user thereof, 
and that no charge shall be exacted from the 
Boeing Company for any use which it may 
make thereof additional to that herein spe- 
cifically provided.”’ (Italics are ours.) 


PR Cera TTY a AME eae Nene eT er Me RS Te wt wearer Rane ate e Mya me Te ah Ce a Nie eT ey OM Tet fa ry 


RoC SA I MERINO 


24 


(4) And paragraph 20 provides that the City will 
‘‘impose no charge of any kind or character 
upon materials and/or products purchased or 
otherwise acquired by the Boeing Company 
for use on the premises herein leased.”’ 


It is important to bear in mind that the above lease 
was entered into approximately six months prior to 
the date upon which the Tax Act was amended to give 
municipalities the taxes upon gasoline collected at their 
airports. Prior to this amendment there was no pos- 
sible basis for a contention that the tax was in the 
nature of an exaction of compensation for the use of 
airports. We therefore have a situation wherein the 
City of Cheyenne, after agreeing upon a fixed compen- 
sation for the use of its airport, claims that it is en- 
titled to additional compensation by reason of legisla- 
tion passed subsequent to the date of its agreement. 
It is at once clear that the imposition of this tax as 
compensation for the use of the airport would consti- 
tute a violation of Section 10 of Article I of the Con- 
stitution of the United States. The obligation of the 
City of Cheyenne to give the respondent the use of 
the airport under the terms provided in the lease 
would be substantially impaired if the City was al- 
lowed to exact compensation from the respondent in 


addition to that provided for in the lease. 


Decisions of this Court make it clear that the ex- 


action of compensation in addition to that provided 


RRR SARA ESE Ue ne os aerate 


25 


in the lease would constitute an impairment of the 
obligation of contract. 


In the ease of Boise Artesian H. d& C. Water Co. 
v. Boise City, 230 U. S. 84, the City had granted a 
franchise authorizing the laying of water pipes in the 
streets of the city, without exacting any compensation 
therefor. The franchise was accepted and the grantees 
and their successors invested a large amount of money 
in the construction of a water system for the purpose 
of supplying inhabitants of the city with water. Sub- 
sequently, the City enacted an ordinance requiring the 
Water Company to pay a license fee of $300 a month 
for the privilege of laying and maintaining its water 
pipes in the streets. This Court held that the ordinance 
imposing the license fee impaired the obligation of the 
franchise contract under which the water pipes were 


maintained in the streets, and was therefore void. 


In the above case there was no express provision 
in the ordinance prohibiting the imposition of addi- 
tional charges. In the instant case the impairment of 
the obligation of contract is even more apparent than 
in the above case because the respondent and the City 
of Cheyenne expressly agreed that no additional 
charges for the use of the airport would be imposed. 
If the tax imposed on gasoline taken into respondent’s 
planes on the Cheyenne airport for use in interstate 


commerce is construed as an exaction of compensation 


Ce A 


POS! RE SSE EE SOG AIEEE MM MES pM ty 


2 


Petts ids ate ghee Ey es erecting xt 


2 IP DERE LAIR, Se . 


26 


for the use of that airport, the imposition of the tax 
unquestionably impairs the obligation of that contract 
between the respondent and the City of Cheyenne, 
dated July 1, 1929, and entered into prior to the effec- 


tive date of the statute under which the tax is imposed. 


Dartmouth College v. Woodward, 4 Wheat. 518; 
Murray v. Charleston, 96 U.S. 432; 
Coolidge v. Long, 282 U.S. 582; 


Carondelet Canal Co. v. Louisiana, 233 U. S. 
362. 


CONCLUSION. 


The Circuit Court of Appeals was of the opinion 
that the Wyoming tax did not apply to gasoline 
brought into Wyoming by respondent for use in re- 
spondent’s planes in interstate commerce. If the view 
of the Cireuit Court is adopted by this Court, the 
necessity of a decision upon the validity of the Wvo- 
ming statute, as applied to such gasoline, is avoided. 
If the tax does apply to such gasoline, it falls upon 
the use of the gasoline in interstate commerce. It is 
clear from the language of the statute and the facts 
in this case that it is the actual consumption of the 
gasoline upon which the tax is imposed. Under the 
decision of Helson v. Kentucky, supra, such a tax im- 


poses an unlawful burden upon interstate commerce. 


The contention raised by the petitioners, but not 


pressed, that the tax in question is an exaction of 


. ee 4 
rst aece ite 
ee a 


27 


compensation for the use of the municipal airports at 
Cheyenne and Rock Springs, is without merit. The 
power to exact compensation for the use of these air- 
ports is in the municipalities owning them and not in 
the State of Wyoming. Furthermore, under the de- 
cisions of this Court, there is not a sufficient relation 
between the tax and the use made of the airports to jus- 
tify holding that the tax is a charge made merely as 
compensation for such use. In any event, if the tax is 
construed as an exaction of compensation for the use 
of the Cheyenne Airport it is in violation of Section 10 
of Article I of the Constitution of the United States, 
because it impairs the obligation of the lease between 
the respondent and the City of Cheyenne entered into 
prior to the effective date of the statute under which 


the tax is imposed. 


Respectfully submitted, 


JOHN W. LACEY, 


Cheyenne, Wyoming, 


ELMER E. TODD, 

FRANK E. HOLMAN, 
WILLIAM M. ALLEN, 
CLARENCE R. INNIS, 


Seattle, Washington, 


Attorneys for Respondent,

APPENDIX 
SESSION LAWS OF WYOMING, 1923. 
CHAPTER 73 
HOUSE BILL NO. 70. 
GASOLINE TAX. 


AN ACT providing a license tax on gasoline, providing 
for the collection thereof, defining certain words or 
phrases, prescribing manner and methods of payment, 
fixing penalties for failure to make same, and provid- 
ing for the disposition of said taxes. 


BE IT ENACTED BY THE LEGISLATURE OF THE 
STATE OF WYOMING: 


DEFINITIONS. 

Section 1. The words and phrases used in this Act 
shall be construed for the purpose thereof, as follows: (1) 
‘*Wholesaler’’ is hereby defined as any person, firm or cor- 
poration who imports or causes to be imported gasoline, 
as herein defined, for sale in the State of Wyoming to the 
jobber or consumer, or to the persons, firms, corporations 
or associations of persons, who, in turn, sell to the jobber 
or consumer. The term ‘‘wholesaler’’ is further defined 
to mean any person, firm or corporation who produces, re- 
fines, manufacturers, blends or compounds gasoline in the 
State of Wyoming for use, sale or distribution in this State. 
(2) ‘*Gasoline’’ is hereby defined as the volatile substance 
produced from petroleum, natural gas, oil shales or coal, 
heretofore sold under the name of gasoline, and such other 
volatile and inflammable liquids produced or compounded 
for the purpose of operating or propelling motor vehicles, 


except the product commonly known as kerosene. 


29 


AMOUNT OF TAX. 

Section 2. A license tax of one cent per gallon is here- 
by levied on all gasoline sold in this State after March 1, 
1923, for domestic consumption, to be collected as herein- 
after set forth: 


REPORT—PAYMENT OF TAX. 

Section 3. On and after May Ist, 1923, each and every 
wholesaler as defined in this Act, who is now engaged or 
who may hereafter engage in his own name, or in the name 
of others, or in the name of his representatives or agents, 
in this State, in the sale or use of gasoline as herein de- 
fined, shall not later than the fifteenth of each month render 
to the State Treasurer a statement of all gasoline sold or 
used by them in the State of Wyoming during the preced- 
ing calendar month, and pay to the State Treasurer at the 
same time, the license tax of one cent per gallon on all 
such gasoline. Said statement to the State Treasurer shall 
he upon blanks furnished by him, and shall be sworn to 
by the owner or managing agent in the ease of an indi- 
vidual, firm, or association, by the resident general agent 
or attorney, in the case of a foreign corporation, or by one 


of the principal officers in the case of domestic corporation. 


RECORD BY WHOLESALER. 

Section 4. Every wholesaler in gasoline as in this Act 
defined shall keep a record of all purchases, receipts, sales 
and distribution of gasoline; such records shall at all times 
he subject to inspection by the State Treasurer or his dep- 
uties or such other officers duly authorized by the State 
Treasurer. 

FUNDS USED FOR MAINTENANCE OF TLIGHWAYS. 

Section 5. All funds received by the State Treasurer 


from the gasoline license tax provided in this Act, shall be 


| 


+ oon 
30 


placed to the credit of the State Highway Fund for main- 
tenance, and shall be apportioned between the several coun- 
ties of the State in proportion to the number of miles of 
designated State Highways therein. 


VIOLATION—PENALTY, 

Section 6. It shall be unlawful for any such wholesaler, 
as herein defined, of gasoline, or other products subject to 
said tax, to sell or offer the same for sale in the State of 
Wyoming while delinquent in the payment of the whole or 
any part of the tax due and payable hereunder, and any 
such wholesaler who fails or refuses to pay the tax herein- 
before provided for at the time the same becomes due, shall 
be liable for double the amount thereof to be recovered in 
a suit instituted by and in the name of the State of Wyo- 
ming in any court of competent jurisdiction, in which suit, 
upon application made by the State of Wyoming, a writ of 
injunction may be issued against the defendant or defend- 
ants without requiring any bond, enjoining and restraining 
them from selling or attempting to sell or offer for sale in 
the State of Wyoming any gasoline or other products sub- 
ject to said tax, while the whole or any part of the tax due 
from such defendant remains unpaid, and in such suit the 
court may, upon application made by the State of Wyoming, 
appoint a receiver of the property and business of the de- 


fendant, for the purpose of impounding the same as secu- 
rity for the payment of said delinquent tax and any judg- 
ment recovered in said suit. The State Treasurer or his as- 
sistant or deputies shall have the right, during business 
hours, to inspect the books and records of any party sub- 
ject to said tax, for the purpose of determining the amount 
of the tax due and payable and whether the same has been 
paid. Any party violating any of the provisions of this 
Act shall be guilty of a misdemeanor, and upon conviction

thereof by any court of competent jurisdiction shall be sub- 
ject to a fine of not less than five hundred dollars and not 
more than one thousand dollars, or to imprisonment for six 
months, or both such fine and imprisonment. The remedies 
herein provided shall be cumulative. 


VALIDITY. 

Section 7. The invalidity of any section, subdivision, 
paragraph, provision, sentence, or clause of this Act shall 
not in any manner effect the validity of the remaining por- 
tions thereof. 


IXCEPTION. 
Section 8. Said gasoline license tax shall not be im- 


posed on gasoline when exported or sold for exportation 
from the State of Wyoming to any other State or Nation. 


PROSECUTION. 

Section 9. Upon request of the State Treasurer, it shall 
he the duty of the Attorney General to commence and prose- 
cute to final determination in any court of competent juris- 
diction, any and all necessary action or actions at law, to 
enforce the provisions of this Act. 

Section 10, This Act shall take effect and be in force 
from and after the first day of April, 1923. 


Approved February 26, 1923.

GASOLINE TAX.

March 1, 1927, for domestic consumption to be collected 
as hereinafter set forth.’’ 

Section 2. Section 3, Chapter 73, Session Laws of 
Wyoming, 1923, as amended and re-enacted by Section 2, 
Chapter 89, Session Laws of Wyoming, 1925, is hereby 


amended and re-enacted to read as follows: 
PAYMENT OF TAX.

wholesaler as defined in this Act, who is now engaged or

such fine and imprisonment.”’

CHAPTER 14. 

HOUSE BILL NO. 6. 

PAYMENT OF GASOLINE TAX. 


AN ACT to amend and re-enact Section 3, Chapter 73, 
Session Laws of Wyoming, 1923, as amended and re- 
enacted by Section 2, Chapter 70, Session Laws of 
Wyoming, 1927, as amended and re-enacted by Section 
2, Chapter 139, Session Laws of Wyoming, 1929, pro- 
viding for a license tax on gasoline and for the col- 
lection thereof and providing for statements of gaso- 
line sold or used by wholesalers or users and the pay- 
ment of the tax by them; and providing for the dis- 
position of the tax from gasoline used on municipal 
air fields and fixing penalties for violation of any of the 
provisions of this Act. 


BE IT ENACTED BY THE LEGISLATURE OF THE 

STATE OF WYOMING: 

Section 1. Section 3, Chapter 73, Session Laws of 
Wyoming, 1925, as amended and re-enacted by Section 2, 
Chapter 70, Session Laws of 1927, as amended and re- 
enacted by Section 2, Chapter 139, Session Laws of Wyo- 
ming, 1929, is hereby amended and re-enacted to read 
as follows: 

‘Section 3. On and after April 1, 1929, each and every 
wholesaler as defined in this Act, who is now engaged or 
who may hereafter engage in his own name, or in the name 
of others. or in the name of his representatives or agents 
in this State, in the sale or use of gasoline as herein defined, 
shall not later than the fifteenth of each month, render to 
the State Treasurer a statement of all gasoline sold or used 
by them in the State of Wyoming during the preceding 
calendar month, and pay to the State Treasurer at the same

time, the license tax of four cents per gallon on all such 
gasoline. Said statement to the State Treasurer shall be 
upon blanks furnished by him, and shall be sworn to by the 
owner or managing agent in the case of an individual, firm 
or association, by the resident general agent or attorney, in 
the case of a foreign corporation or by one of the principal 
officers in the case of a domestic corporation. Every person, 
firm or corporation, who shall use any gasoline in this State, 
upon which the said tax has not been paid by any whole- 
saler in this State, shall, on or before the 15th day of each 
calendar month, beginning with the first calendar month 
after this Act has become effective, render a true statement 
to the State Treasurer, duly signed and sworn to, and ac- 
company such statement with the payment of a tax of four 
cents per gallon on all gasoline as shown by such statement 
to have been used by him; provided that before making the 
distribution of funds provided for in Section 1 hereof, the 
State Treasurer shall pay over all funds received from the 
gasoline license tax on gasoline used at any municipal air 
field to the city or town where such air field is located, to 
be used for the maintenance and improvement of such air 
field.’’ 

Section 2. Any party violating any of the provisions 
of this Act shall be guilty of a misdemeanor, and upon con- 
viction thereof by any court of competent jurisdiction, shall 
be subject to a fine of not less than fifty dollars and not 
more than two hundred dollars, or to imprisonment for one 
month, or to both such fine and imprisonment. 

Section 3. All Acts or parts of Acts in conflict with this 
Act are hereby repealed. 

Section 4. This Act shall take effect and be in force 
from and after its passage. 


Approved December 23, 1929.

SUPREME COURT OF THE UNITED STATES.

William H. Edelman, State Treasurer ;| 
Harry R. Weston, State Treasurer;! On Writ of Certiorari to

tioners, cuit Court of Appeals 
vs. for the Tenth Circuit. 


Boeing Air Transport, Ine. 
[April 17, 1933.] 


*, Justice pred delivered _ opinion of the Court. 


acl Resi w Blew ; 

Respondent, a rporation operating airplanes in 
Wyoming, ROA this suit in equity in the District Court of 
Wyoming against petitioners, state tax officials, and the cities of 
Cheyenne and Rock Springs, to enjoin the collection of a state ex- 
cise tax levied upon the use of gasoline by respondent within the 
State, as a violation of the commerce clause of the Constitution. 
As respondent waived relief by interlocutory injunction, see Smith 
vy. Wilson, 273 U. S. 388, 391, the case was tried before a single 
judge who upheld the tax and dismissed the case on the merits. 
51 F. (2d) 130. On appeal the Court of Appeals for the Tenth 
Circuit reversed the decree and directed that the petitioners be 
enjoined from assessing the tax on gasoline procured by respon- 
dent by ‘‘purchases completed outside the State of Wyoming 
and then brought into that State and used in its planes in inter- 
state commerce’’. 61 F. (2d) 319. This Court granted certiorari. 


—U.S 
The statute’ levies a ‘‘license tax of four cents per gallon 
on all gasoline used or sold in this State . . . for domestic con- 


sumption’’ and requires every ‘‘wholesaler’’ engaged in the ‘‘sale 
or use of gasoline’’ within the State to report to the State treasurer 
each month all the gasoline ‘‘sold or used’’ by it in the State, and 
to pay the tax upon it. Gasoline ‘‘exported or sold for exportation 


1Wyoming Laws of 1929, c. 14, amending Laws of 1929, ce. 139; Laws of 
1927, c. 70; Laws of 1925, ec. 89; Laws of 1923, ¢. 73. 


a 


2 Edelman et al. vs. Boeing Air Transport, Inc. 


from the State’’ is exempted from the tax. <A ‘‘wholesaler’’ is de- 
fined as any person (1) who ‘‘imports or causes to be imported gas- 
oline . . . forsale in the State . . . to the jobber or consumer, 
or to the persons . . . who, in turn, sell to the jobber or consumer’’, 
or (2) who ‘‘ produces, refines, manufactures, blends or compounds 
gasoline’’ in Wyoming ‘‘for use, sale or distribution in this State’’. 
In addition the statute provides that ‘‘every person . . who 
shall use any gasoline in this State upon which the said oon has 
not been paid by any wholesaler in this State,’’ shall render a like 
statement and pay a like tax. 

Respondent maintains an airplane service for the transportation 
in interstate commerce of passengers, mail and express with airports 
at Cheyenne and Rock Springs. It purchases gasoline both within 
and without the State, which it intermingles and stores in tanks 
at the two airports. It pays the tax without objection omé#hepase- 
pt been pais bethe- erhetteater-tasrelies on all gasoline which it 
sells within the State at its airports or withdraws from the tanks 
for local use. But it contends, and the court below held, that the 
tax cannot validly be applied to the gasoline imported from out- 
side the State, stored in tanks at the airports and used for ‘‘filling”’ 
the interstate airplanes in which it is eventually consumed. 

The opinion below leaves us uncertain whether the injunction 
was granted upon the ground that the taxing statute does not, in 
any event, apply to gasoline purchased without the state, if not 
sold within it, or upon the ground that the taxation of gasoline 
purchased outside the state and used at respondent’s airports to 
‘*fill’’ its interstate planes, though authorized by the statute, is a 
prohibited burden on interstate commerce. But the bill of complaint 
sought no relief on the ground that the statute does not apply to 
the gasoline so used and no question of the applicability of the tax 
is argued here. Issue is joined on the only question raised by the 
pleadings, whether the taxation of the gasoline which respondent 
withdraws from storage and uses for ‘‘filling’’ its planes imposes 
an unconstitutional burden on interstate commerce. Hence we 
confine our decision to that question. 

As the statute has been administratively construed and applied, 
the tax is not levied upon the consumption of gasoline in furnish- 
ing motive power for respondent’s interstate planes. The tax is ap- 


Ee LE BLT IE IE OF IT TR LL I Es TEEN —— 


—— 


Edelman et al. vs. Boeing Air Transport, Inc. 3 


plied to the stored gasoline as it is withdrawn from the storage tanks 
at the airport and placed in the planes. No tax is collected for 
gasoline consumed in respondent ’s planes either on coming into the 
State or on going out. It is at the time of withdrawal alone that 
‘“gse’’ is measured for the purposes of the tax. The stored gasoline 
is deemed to be ‘‘used’’ within the State and therefore subject to 
the tax, when it is withdrawn from the tanks. Compare No. 176, 
Nashville, Chattanooga & St. Louis Ry. v. Wallace, decided Febru- 
ary 6, 1933; Gregg Dyeing Co. v. Query, 286 U. 8. 472; Hart Re- 
fineries v. Harmon, 278 U. 8. 499; Bowman v. Continental Ou Co., 
256 U. S. 642. 

A State may validly tax the ‘‘use’’ to which gasoline is put in 
withdrawing it from storage within the State, and placing it 
in the tanks of the planes, notwithstanding that its ultimate 
function is to generate motive power for carrying on interstate 
ecommerce. Such a tax cannot be distinguished from that con- 
sidered and upheld in No. 176, Nashville, Chattanooga & St. 


Louis Ry. v. Wallace, supra. There it was pointed out that ‘‘there %, 
can be no valid objection to the taxation of the exercise of e 
any right or power incident to . . . ownership of the gaso- =) 
line which falls short of a tax directly imposed on its use in inter- eo 


state commerce, deemed forbidden in Helson v. Kentucky’’, 279 #3 
U. S. 245. As the exercise of the powers taxed, the storage and Sis 
withdrawal from storage of the gasoline, was complete before inter- ‘ 
state commerce began, it was held that the burden of the tax was f 
too indirect and remote from the function of interstate commerce, 2 
to transgress constitutional limitations. 4 

Despite the fact that the statute as applied is identical in 
operation with that sustained in No. 176, Nashville, Chattanooga 
& St. Louis Ry. v. Wallace, supra, respondent contends that as the 
statute is written, the tax is one on the consumption of gasoline in 
propelling its airplanes in interstate commerce, invalid under 
Helson v. Kentucky, supra. In that case a Kentucky statute taxing 
the use of gasoline was applied to that purchased and placed in the 
tanks of a ferry boat outside the State for use in operating it in 
interstate commerce. The tax, which was levied only with respect 
to the gasoline consumed while the ferry boat was within the State, 
was held to be invalid as, in effect, a direct tax on the privilege of 
carrying on interstate commerce. 


SEI eb aera peer eters ee PRE Ao EINE FEES Ut 4 2 PE a 


Edelman et al. vs. Boeing Atr Transport, Inc. 


But the officers of Wyoming, charged with the enforcement of 
the taxing statute, are giving no such application to it as was 
given to that in Helson v. Kentucky, supra, and it is not suggested 
that they will. All that has been done or threatened by them, 
under their interpretation of the statute, infringes no constitutional 
right of the complainant. In the circumstances, no case is pre- 
sented, either by pleadings or proof, calling on a federal court of 
equity to rule upon the correctness of some other construction 
which may never be adopted by the state administrative officials 
or by the state courts.

Reversed.
